- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i share the rapporteur's concern that the european institutions seem to be failing to put in place clear and understandable rules which are transparent, more easily accessible and for people with a normal iq level. the rapporteur has the merit of putting his finger on the wound. it is rightly emphasized that the over-complicated regulations and red tape are seen by citizens and businesses in the european union as evidence of the inefficiency of the institutions. the causes are obvious. until a clear centralised approach is developed within the commission in the area of slim, the slim initiative will remain a non-binding exercise. the latter certainly cannot be. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. firstly, the slim initiative must be made mandatory, particularly in view of the forthcoming enlargement of the european union. secondly, member states must now set the example by ensuring that european directives are transposed correctly and in a timely manner. the impression has been given all too often that the much needed simplification of the rules is a procession of echternach. the european union's enlargement is a matter of accelerating the pace of the internal market in a professional and responsible manner. i am glad that the member state which i have the honour to represent here has recognised the need to take special measures, as is the case in the netherlands, to achieve consistent simplification of the rules. the liberal group is convinced of the need to simplify the rules and will support the doorn report wholeheartedly.
- mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, education is one of those areas of responsibility which is and must be closely linked to the life of a community. the commission's proposal is to be implemented as closely as possible to the population, and that means that there will never be a general european education system. but precisely because education is so important in the transfer, not only of knowledge and not only in the acquisition of skills, but also in the transfer of culture, in the insertion of the individual into the social fabric, good education is very important. we are therefore spending a lot of money on it, and yet there is a growing need for european quality control, and we would like to support you in this. parents know that education is a great effort for them, for the children and for the students, but at the same time they want results. in the past, it might have been enough to go to a good school because that good school also had your ideological trust. that's not enough anymore. parents want quality education and children who go to school want to have learned what is really important in life. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. in any case, it has led to a high degree of self-evaluation in schools in flanders, which spontaneously feel that they have to defend themselves in this field of education. on the other hand, there is a growing concern that is common. for example, in flanders, it used to be a bit taboo to talk about quality control, because there was a fear in the various educational networks that this could create hostility. on the contrary, we now see that quality assurance is increasingly becoming a common concern, thanks to the fact that quality assurance in education is also a gateway to this great european community and to the labour market of this european community. today i can show you the results of a whole survey carried out in flemish education to see whether both primary and secondary education meet the common standards. it is therefore important to note how much importance the media attach to this, precisely because they know that the parents, the students and the whole teaching community are watching these results very closely. because every school, every educational network, wants to do well, but at the same time, every parent wants their child to go to a good school. i note that the complaints about the lack of resources are also being fed from the educational field, because we not only want our children to learn well in school, we also want that school to be well equipped, to be a safe environment, in other words, to have the infrastructure up to par. unfortunately, in many countries, this still requires a lot of resources.

motivated, and i would like to emphasize this here. the criticism of the court of auditors, the follow-up of parliament, has made this happen. moreover, the commission, under pressure from the court of auditors, has pursued a policy of deconcentration which has given the people in the delegation in sarajevo, as well as in other delegations, greater clout, enabled the head of delegation to respond more effectively to the needs on the ground and, among other things, has ensured that all overdue payments have now been eliminated. so, on this point, good points for the commission. another financial director who has written a whole book of rules to follow. there was apparently a certain amount of waste at the beginning of the high representative's term. the efforts of this man, the efforts of the commission, the efforts of the bureau as a whole have resulted in the budget of the office of the high representative being reduced by almost eur 5 million in three years. i think this is a very positive result. one of the main criticisms of the high representative was also paragraph 17 on the former financial director's confusion of interests. parliament, at least in the resolution - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking mrs ria oomen-ruijten for the good cooperation she has shown in the report. i think it was a good idea to re-examine, in an own-initiative report, some of the demands made by this parliament in 1998 following my own report on the basis of a petition by frontier workers. in the legal field, not much has happened in those three years. we have often said that border regions are a barometer of european integration. if that is the case, then i think that the situation with regard to integration is not very good today, because little progress has been made, and only the european court of justice has made things happen. i also regret that the frontier impact assessment has not even been included in the social agenda and that the treaty of nice has not brought us any progress in the decision-making on regulation 1408/71, which only makes matters more difficult. in the meantime, the world is not standing still, and tax reforms are under way in all possible countries, social security legislation is in full swing, legal systems are being supplemented by complementary systems and there are many countries which, in the context of reconciling work, family and leisure, invent all kinds of possible career interruption schemes which then apply only to their own residents and unfortunately do not apply to frontier workers. i therefore give the european commission the right to open an inquiry into the belgian system of career interruption which unfortunately does not apply to the dutch, whereas the dutch system does apply to the belgians. border workers are at a supplementary disadvantage compared with other citizens who benefit from the free movement of persons. often they work in one country and live in another, paying taxes in one country and social security contributions in another. if we hope for some coherence between fiscal policy and social security policy in one country, it is utopian to think that this is happening between countries and that this is causing permanent disadvantages for frontier workers. even the most convinced european federalists among us dare not even dream of the utopia that there would ever be a harmonized social security system or harmonized taxation in a european context. so we have to find other creative solutions. i would like to quote two of them. the oomen-ruijten report rightly calls for the european parliament to make it compulsory for member states to report on the effects of any changes to social security and tax legislation at the frontiers, and to compensate for the disadvantages that would be caused to frontier workers, a practice which is being used in some countries to a disastrous extent. my group therefore has some problems with the weakening of the text at the time by mrs oomen-ruijten. we believe that the test must be maintained as a matter of urgency and that compensation should be made compulsory. secondly, we must dare to go a little further than the conclusions of 1998. we were unable to turn off the russian roulette for the border workers. we must finally choose between one taxation principle, either the country of residence or the country of work. i think there are plenty of arguments for choosing the country of work. i think that my group will therefore vote against the amendments tabled by mrs oomen-ruijten and against those tabled by mr manders who want to remove this principle. i hope that this report will come out of the plenary intact and that the frontier workers will finally take a step forward.

- madam president, mr president-in-office, madam commissioner, mr high representative, i am glad that this debate is finally taking place. in april 1999, when the nato bombing began, we had tried to engage him in the previous parliament. but it didn't work. a few days later, on 5 may, i and 11 colleagues sent a letter to mr solana, then secretary-general of nato, because we were certain that this type of weapon was being used, asking him to put an end to its use, since it was a weapon that could subsequently cause health problems for the population, the military, not to mention irreversible contamination of the environment. we did not receive an answer at the time. i think that deserves to be emphasized. nato has provided a trickle of information; i can only take this as proof of the fact that the un environment programme working group found at the end of 1999 that it did not have sufficient information to assess the state of depleted uranium contamination in kosovo. what mr solana has just said to us a few moments ago, that we are going to act in a transparent manner, is new, it is a good thing, but i have some doubts, given the position adopted by nato for a year on this desire for transparency. i am even more surprised when i hear what the current nato representatives are saying. and i would like to clarify a number of points. because i think it's necessary. when it is said that there is a link between the health of the military and the presence or absence of depleted uranium, one must be careful because it is not easy; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the health of a number of military personnel is not compromised. there is chemical pollution, there is a whole range of environmental damage, there are living conditions. it is also known that radioactive contamination does not show its effects until a relatively long period has elapsed. it is therefore not correct to say, as some scientists do, in what i believe is a light-hearted way, that "there is no link". it's too soon. i'm sure that's true for some military men, but i'd be surprised if it could be generalized. secondly, depleted uranium is not something natural, as some people try to make us believe, or less radioactive. it is about 20% less radioactive than natural uranium. it's a waste product of the nuclear industry. it should be treated as such. and i would point out that we have a european directive, which dates from 1996, and which should be transposed by the member states, which provides that from a concentration of 10 000 becquerels per kilo, such waste must be confined. but here we are at a concentration 4,000 times higher. so it is completely absurd to use such a munition to spread it into the environment, to create insoluble particles that end up in the respiratory tract and in the digestive tract, to claim that we have to prove that there is a problem. but there must be a problem, or else i no longer understand the european directive. why should what is valid for the people of europe not be valid for the people of kosovo and bosnia and for the military? in writing. - the council's proposal is welcome in line with the conclusions of the european councils of cologne, helsinki and feira, which called on the union to 'improve and use more effectively the means at its disposal for civilian crisis management'. the union has the experience and means to implement operations such as election control, border management, training and equipping of police forces as quickly as possible. these potentials should be exploited, especially since the union was not able to react quickly enough and the financing mechanisms for such operations were not flexible enough. the european commission does not provide for a geographical limit to the rapid reaction system (rrm). it does, however, provide that interventions must be of short duration. if events require longer intervention, it will be appropriate to use the means currently available such as thematic programmes. for example, under the meda programme, the drr will be used for training immigration officers and for cooperation in the fight against terrorism.
in writing. (de) the search for practical solutions to the problems of the 420 000 frontier workers implies recognition of the internal market and the free movement of persons guaranteed by it. i therefore welcome with enthusiasm the fact that mrs oomen-ruijten's report addresses a number of these problems, including their origin, and at the same time proposes measures to eliminate the disparities between tax and social security systems for the people concerned. the contradictions between the legal provisions of each member state and the principle of free movement are above all a reminder that regulation 1408/71 is no longer appropriate and can no longer offer legal security to workers affected by the many amendments. the lack of coordination has a direct consequence in that frontier workers are often discriminated against in family allowances, unemployment, pensions and healthcare. in the latter area, i welcome the initiatives of healthcare sector actors to offer 'exportable' services which take into account the specific situation of frontier workers. as in the social and healthcare sectors, there are also several legal uncertainties in the tax area, which have not been addressed by bilateral tax agreements concluded by member states. this situation creates inequalities for both frontier workers and local authorities which, in the near future, will only be corrected by a coordination of tax systems at european level. the measure proposed by mrs oomen-ruijten to oblige member states, by means of a directive, to examine the effects on frontier workers of amending their tax and social security legislation and to create compensation rules at the same time can only be supported. despite the european parliament's many statements in favour of harmonizing taxation and social security legislation and despite the commission's concrete proposals to modernise and simplify regulation 1408/71, it is the member states' ministers themselves who, in the council of ministers, block this necessary reform process and prevent the completion of the internal market for persons.
in writing. - the explanatory memorandum expresses the legitimate concern about this important issue of the health of soldiers in difficult areas. the people concerned are rightly interested, including in the conditions which must be met for the social, economic and environmental reconstruction of the affected regions. so on the whole, the resolution is fair and i am very pleased with it. on the other hand, paragraph 6 calling on nato member states to propose a moratorium is, as it stands, excessive, especially since the precautionary principle is violated by the draft which calls on nato to consider other types of munitions until the results of the depleted uranium investigation are known. i therefore decided not to vote for paragraph 6 and to abstain on the whole text.
- mr president, in previous debates on the human rights situation, i have always called for the european union to take more preventive action and to play less of a game. i am therefore somewhat surprised that neither the presidency nor the commission, in the introduction just now, said a word about the human rights situation in africa. i therefore take the liberty of drawing your attention in advance to a very current and potentially very explosive human rights situation, in particular the situation in the congo following the assassination of president kabila. despite the confusing and often contradictory information, it is becoming increasingly clear that a power vacuum threatens to exist in kinshasa. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. at the same time, i believe that all the countries directly or indirectly involved in the conflict should be strongly urged not to interfere in the present internal crisis. if the neighbouring countries do not keep their heads down, the crisis could escalate further with all the possible consequences. it is also important that the lusaka peace process be taken up quickly and that the inter-congolese dialogue be able to start with the cooperation of all those concerned. i say that in the light of the 1994 rwandan genocide, in which the international community had to watch hundreds of thousands of people being killed, and in which i would like to call for greater assertiveness on the part of the european union and greater vigilance, including on the part of the un, when it comes to central africa. and so i come to the agenda of the un human rights commission in geneva. despite the many conventions and agreements, the rights of women, children and other vulnerable groups are still being violated on a large scale. worldwide, one in three women is physically abused. fifty million children work in unacceptable conditions and two million girls under 15 are forced into prostitution every year. i would like to draw particular attention to two types of human rights violations which have taken on alarming proportions in europe in recent years. on the one hand, the phenomenon of paedophilia and child pornography. while we rightly criticize asian countries for their child labour or african countries for their child soldiers, here in western europe we are regularly shocked by paedophilia and the child pornography scandals. i refer to the excellent report by mr kirkhope. two new revelations show the seriousness of the situation. in italy a network was recently launched that offered images of child sex and cruelty for sale on the internet. last week, british police managed to dismantle the wonderland club, which seized 750,000 indecent images of children. a second disturbing phenomenon is human trafficking. every year, some 30 million people are trafficked to other countries, half a million of them to the european union. human trafficking has become a highly lucrative international industry, often with deadly risks to the passengers. so many bodies of drowned illegals wash up on spanish beaches that a macabre debate has arisen over who should pay for the funerals. in both cases, the desperation of poor people is exploited by unscrupulous money-mongers. in both cases, information in the countries of origin and coordination of international police services are necessary to combat these abuses. sweden has a good reputation when it comes to defending human rights in the world. i really hope that in the next six months mr zeden will live up to his reputation and implement the recommendations made by parliament in these two important areas.

- mr president, when i started in politics some thirty years ago and went to the preparatory meeting of the world conference on women in mexico, which was held in new york, i accompanied the only female diplomat we had in our country at that time. all the discussions in new york about how we could help women to make their way in decision-making, in the workplace, and so on, escaped her. for she had achieved her goal in her own strength, but she had had to give up so much to become a woman i do not wish other women to take as a model. because women who made their way into politics, into diplomacy, and so on many years ago, are women who have had to leave behind a lot. what we want is to give women the opportunity to make their way in a normal way with some help from society, including in decision-making, in politics and in the world of work. and then one can say: how is this to be done? how can we help women? we have been looking for years for strategies for a society which says that women should have all the opportunities, but has so little thought about stringent effective strategies to achieve this. the few strategies that were found have always been strategies with targets or quotas. you can call it whatever you want. the target system or the quota system is not a system that applies only to women. in my country, where the two language areas, dutch and french, must live in good relations with each other, we also apply quotas for the language relations. so the quota system is not a system specifically for women. it is now being treated as if it were a system that applies only to women, but it is a system that is applied in many areas of society. it may be applied in other fields in the future. so i gave one example. i don't understand people who are so afraid of this system. i am constantly asking myself whether it is to prevent women from entering that we are against the quota system, or whether we are really acting against the substance of the matter. i am in favour of a quota system. i am not followed by a large part of my group, but by a number of others. i'm all for the system. i successfully introduced it in belgium as the only country in europe, followed by france. i think it is one of the few practical ways that exists at the moment. i support the karamanou report. - thank you.

the rapporteur. - mr president, i shall take more than five minutes because the speakers in my group who have withdrawn have left 15 minutes. so i can take 20 minutes. so i shall speak very slowly, mr president, because i think you do not intend to prevent a real debate and therefore a real presentation of each of the arguments.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the initiative of the french republic which is the subject of my report concerns third-country nationals who hold what is known as a national long-stay visa - that is to say, a visa valid for no more than three months - issued by a member state of the schengen area. whose nationals are awaiting the issue of a residence permit in the member state which has been willing to take them in. in the present state of community law, that is to say, in particular, article 18 of the schengen implementing convention, this third-country national can only transit through the territory of the other member states to the country which issued the long-stay visa. and once in the country, that national can no longer move around the schengen area until he has obtained what is known as his permanent residence permit. however, and this is precisely the problem, the issue of a permanent residence permit can take several weeks, if not months, mainly because of the burden of administrative procedures, the poor coordination of administrative services in some member states and the difficulty for the person concerned to know precisely the procedure to be followed. as a result, and this concerns thousands of people every year on the territory of the union, the holder of a long-stay visa, who is awaiting a definitive residence permit, is the one who is, from the point of view of freedom of movement in the schengen area, in the most unfavourable position compared to the nationals of other third countries in terms of freedom of movement. this situation is all the more paradoxical and, i would say, all the more scandalous, since obtaining a national long-stay visa is subject in each of our countries to a set of extremely strict conditions. apart from family reunification cases, applicants are subject to a thorough preliminary investigation. they must show that they have sufficient resources and produce, as the case may be, either a certificate certifying their acceptance into an educational or research institution or a formal employment contract. the objective pursued by the french republic is simple and is very clearly stated in the explanatory memorandum. the aim is to make it possible for holders of a long-stay national visa to move freely within the schengen area while awaiting their final residence permit. and we all share, i hope, the objective of facilitating the free movement of these citizens. the problem is not therefore with the objective pursued but with the means chosen to achieve it and the consequences that these imply in terms of the discretionary powers conferred on the member states in relation to the rights of citizens. the french republic's initiative has chosen, rather surprisingly, articles 62.2 and 63.3 as its legal basis, both of which concern the procedures and conditions for issuing visas. if the real objective is to facilitate the free movement of nationals, the appropriate legal basis is article 62 (3), which provides that the council is to adopt, within five years of the entry into force of the treaty of amsterdam, measures laying down the conditions under which nationals of third countries may move freely within the territory of the member states for a maximum period of three months. the choice of legal basis is never an innocent choice. in reality, the legal basis adopted in the french initiative shows that the priority objective of this initiative is to harmonise the conditions for issuing long-stay visas by assimilating them to uniform short-stay visas. this means that if the proposal as presented by the french republic were accepted, it would give all the member states implementing enhanced schengen cooperation the right, through what is known as prior consultation, to be informed of any long-stay visa applications in any other schengen country and could therefore impede the free movement of applicants who are not accepted by that country. in practice, this means that if this arrangement were accepted, if spain decided tomorrow to grant a long-stay visa to an algerian national, through the prior consultation procedure, the french state could ask to be systematically informed, and it would have the right to oppose this without anyone knowing, because no one now knows which countries are using the prior consultation procedure, and the council refuses to communicate this list of countries. i have therefore chosen to amend the french republic's initiative to bring it into line with its stated objective, namely that, proclaimed in the explanatory memorandum, of facilitating the movement within the schengen area of holders of a national residence visa who are awaiting their residence permit. i also support the objective of introducing a uniform long-stay visa, as is already the case for short-stay visas. but i hope, and i think we all should hope, that such an initiative, which the commission should be the author of, will be part of a clear, coherent and comprehensive proposal and not, as here, part of a fragmentary initiative in its scope and unbalanced in its mechanism. that is why i ask this house to support the amendments which i have tabled with the unanimous support of the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs.
- mr president, this debate, as others have already said, comes at a crucial moment for the protagonists but also for europe. crucial first because of the upcoming elections for the post of prime minister in israel. this week, time is on the line for the results of the ballot box. the taba talks, which had, however, led to the rejection of the creation of committees, particularly on the border issues and on jerusalem and the palestinian refugees, were also officially suspended. finally, although i noted mr solana's optimism, the possibility of a summit being held this week in stockholm has been suspended following the aggressive, or at least unconstructive, to use diplomatic language, statements made by yasser arafat in davos on sunday. and i was also saying, because of the transition in the united states, that this is a crucial moment: bill clinton is no longer here. the president had made the peace process the top priority of his agenda. the future will now tell how george bush and colin powell view their presence on the ground. for the first time in years, mr danielsson said, high-level negotiations have just taken place in taba without the presence of american officials but with mr moratinos, our envoy to the peace process. this may be europe's chance, a chance to move forward, to take a greater political role in this field, to make its voice heard and to showcase its know-how, because we aspire to be so much more than the banker of the region. we know that our aid is necessary, vital, for the palestinians whose economic situation is desperate, but we want to export much more than euros. we want to export our values in terms of democracy, respect for human rights, education and tolerance. i welcome mr patten's commitment to transparency in the use of our european aid. albert einstein said, "peace cannot be maintained by force, but can be achieved by understanding. let us make europe the vehicle for this understanding.

- mr president, commissioner, mr president-in-office of the council, my speech will focus on the commission's document for the lisbon european council. i think it is an interesting document and the remarks i am going to make are intended to suggest to the commission a number of improvements. it's called constructive criticism. firstly, as regards the content of the document, i am very surprised divided into very different geographical sub-groups. the scandinavian countries and the countries of the south do not share exactly the same reality; this should be taken into account to refine the diagnosis. secondly, the commission rightly speaks of an integrated strategy. i would like to make suggestions in three main areas to improve this integration. first, sustainable development: it is important that the commission recognises that the lisbon process lacks an environmental dimension. it will be necessary to ensure that the gothenburg document rearranges the order of priorities in lisbon. to take a concrete example, it is not enough to say, in the field of energy and transport, that liberalisation reduces prices and therefore offers better access for consumers and businesses; the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. the problem of indicators will arise and that is why we have introduced a number of amendments to the bullmann report. similarly, the commission cannot simply say that it has submitted a communication on services of general interest. parliament is considering it. initiatives will have to be taken to regulate the movements towards competition in the various sectors. secondly, the role of education and training. you start with a fundamental observation, the lack of training. the commission should examine two questions more closely. on the one hand, the organization of alternating periods of living, working and training. why not a framework directive or at least a commission recommendation to enable workers and citizens in general to benefit from alternating rhythms of training, work and life time? on the other hand, there is the problem of public and private financing of this training, and the problem of the responsibility of firms up to and including the training of their workers, which raises the question of the quality of public expenditure. thirdly, i think the commission would do well to follow the example of the report on economic and social cohesion in its document, in which mr barnier anticipated the enlargement of the european union. it is clear that many of the priorities set out in the commission document should be re-formulated in the context of the enlarged union: the labour market, where health and safety will become fundamental issues, social exclusion and social protection, which will not be linked to the question of the ageing population but simply to the establishment of a social safety net, as the ibrd report has just reminded us in its latest edition. these are a series of suggestions, commissioner, which i would like to see taken into account in the commission's future reports, particularly with regard to integration, sustainable development and the knowledge society.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, my speech refers to paragraphs 9 to 12 of the report by our colleague, mrs gasòliba i böhm. my speech will focus on our approach to the concept of universal service. the universal service must be defined in relation to the new technological environment. the current developments - particularly in information and communication technologies, which are all converging towards digital - are an unavoidable catalyst. it is up to the public authorities to meet the challenge by enabling every citizen, whatever his situation, to benefit equally and according to his specific needs from an identical quality of service. that's the advantage of universal service. in fact, technological developments make it possible to think of universal service in an offensive and voluntarist way and not in a defensive and past-oriented way, as a limited set of public services, often poor, which are carefully avoided unless they are cornered. the medium-term prospects offered by the widespread use of new smart cards in europe are thus leading to a redefinition and reorganization of the universal service, which is polarized around two vectors of change: a service level agreement and rich content. what we want is for it to be able to develop effectively in this direction, so that this universal service can contribute to the balanced achievement of what was set as a strategic objective at the extraordinary european council in lisbon in march 2000.

- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, as a member of the committees on budgetary control and consumer affairs i follow closely but with passion the developments in our flawed agricultural policy. the court of auditors' special report on the reform of the sugar market organization is full of surprises, paragraph by paragraph, as to the shortcomings of this market organization. the rapporteur, mr daul, has not said a word about this report from the court of auditors. the new european commission did so: the commission has had the courage to come forward with a proposal for reform and adjustments, but i can see that our committee on agriculture is sweeping these proposals off the table, is in favour of short-term policies and is opting for short-term success. it is capitulating to the interests and the sugar-sweetened lobby. it is a policy which goes against the interests of the farmer himself and is, moreover, directed mainly at industry. i cannot approve this report, ladies and gentlemen. it's a violation of sound financial management. the net cost of overproduction in 2000 was 800 million euro, almost the same amount as the sum we approved in addition to the bse crisis a few weeks ago. the average stock of white sugar is 14% of the production quota or 2 million tonnes. the commission considers that only 0.4 million tonnes, or 3%, is needed to cover unexpected shortfalls. annual costs: 30 million euros. the consumer is also faced with a market in which only five companies hold more than 50% of the eu quota. in most sugar-producing member states, only two companies hold the entire national quota. speaking of competition! this policy is contrary to the interests of consumers, the environment, high-quality production and the third world. i ask you to reject this report and at least to take account of the court of auditors' observations.

the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the main purpose of the initiative presented to us today by the republic of finland is to reserve implementing powers to the council with regard to certain detailed provisions and practical arrangements for examining visa applications. the portuguese initiative actually pursues the same objective, namely to reserve implementing powers to the council, but this time with regard to border control and surveillance. on the fundamental issue, i would ask the house to reject these two initiatives, which in my view contain four serious political errors. firstly, it is a political error, contrary to the spirit of the community and the treaty of amsterdam. when the amsterdam treaty decided to move the schengen acquis from the third to the first pillar, it did so in order to remove the matter from intergovernmental practice and to integrate it into the normal legal and institutional framework. by reserving the implementing powers to itself, the council is in effect denying the spirit of the treaty of amsterdam, perpetuating in practice the intergovernmental methods which had previously been in place. the second mistake of the council is, i would say, institutional. i must remind my colleagues that article 202 of the treaty on european union is not just any article. it is one of the institutional provisions of the treaty in the strict sense, and the court of justice has held that those provisions have genuine primacy. in my view, by reserving the implementing powers to itself, by considering that this is a specific case, which it fails to justify in detail, the council is taking liberties with a fundamental provision of the treaty on european union, which in my view is unacceptable. the third mistake the council has made is with regard to the commission. the council justified its intention of reserving implementing powers to itself by the sensitivity of the visa and border management policy areas. this argument does not stand up to analysis, especially when one knows that the regulation on the uniform format of visas expressly reserves the implementing measures, which are otherwise secret, to the commission, although this is obviously a particularly sensitive matter. i would also like to know how long the commission of the european communities will continue to stand by and watch the council's real devaluation of its powers vis-à-vis it. not only does it exercise the power of initiative in its place, but now it also wants to deprive it, as the treaties require, of the power of execution. the fourth fault concerns our assembly directly. by reserving executive powers to itself - and all members should know this - the council deprives the european parliament of all the information to which it would be entitled in the so-called normal framework of comitology. this refusal to provide information is all the more serious since certain decisions have already been taken and will continue to be taken tomorrow in the framework of procedures which are protected by secrecy. for example, under the so-called prior consultation procedure, certain third-country nationals who want to come to one of the countries of the union, the schengen area, will not be able to have access because a country, whose identity we will never know, and which is not necessarily the country of destination, will have vetoed that person, for reasons we will never know and which will never have to be justified. i do not want to confuse the two by claiming that we should have a say in the individual decisions on each visa application, but i cannot accept that in such fundamental matters as these, which affect the fundamental freedoms of citizens, the council will never be accountable to anyone for its priorities or the criteria for its choices. it is not just a democratic deficit, it is a real denial of democracy. that is why i ask this house, following the unanimous decision of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, to reject outright the initiatives of the republic of finland and the republic of portugal.

- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that there is a great potential for improving energy efficiency in the european union, in all sectors of the economy and also among private users. it is therefore essential to develop an effective strategy. it is the task of the european commission to make proposals to improve building regulations, to promote the energy efficiency of electrical equipment, including the setting of standards for standby function, and last but not least, to propose measures to achieve a community policy for a sustainable transport system. i think that the european parliament too has a role to play in setting an example. why, for example, could solar panels not be installed in the european parliament buildings, both here and in brussels? the energy generated would already be sufficient to supply electricity for the hundreds of computers in the european parliament. energy efficiency is not just a matter of installing more efficient technologies. it is also a matter of patterns of behavior. instead of blindly focusing on raising taxes or introducing a co2 tax, the member states should instead focus on creating incentives for investment in energy efficiency, for example by reducing vat rates on energy-saving equipment and services, introducing a real estate tax reduction for buildings that meet high energy efficiency standards and granting special tax benefits for cogeneration in particular. such measures are a major incentive to promote energy efficiency, and i think we should insist that this be made a real thing.

- mr president, so far conflict prevention has not been a priority. the commission has already made a number of proposals for the future. we look forward to april. the key question here, as mrs lalumière has already said, is whether the european union will be decisive enough when it comes to prevention. in the former yugoslavia we have not been able to prevent the conflicts for a long time. but there have been times when conflict prevention has ended in disaster. i would like to refer to the many african examples, particularly rwanda. the mandates on the basis of which prevention is established must be sufficient to prevent conflict. they must be proactive and allow for crisis management. you are talking, commissioner, about the whole corps we are going to have to put together. but our starting point must be, first of all, a correct understanding of the deeper reasons and origins of the conflicts. the root of evil is often the underdevelopment and neglect or oppression of certain regions, religions, cultures and ethnic minorities. it is a fertile breeding ground for illegal arms trafficking and banditry. so let us not forget integrated development cooperation as a means of addressing the root causes of conflict.
- mr president, free and fair elections are unfortunately still not a reality in many countries. in africa in particular, elections are often marred by intimidation, violence, fraud or the elimination of rivals through constitutional amendments, as recently happened in côte d'ivoire. the sending of observers is therefore very useful, but it can also involve risks. observers can be manipulated and played against each other. they can be used as an alibi to legitimize a regime that came to power illegally. the greatest possible care must therefore be exercised both in the decision to send observers and in the monitoring and reporting on the spot. now there's too much improvising. people are sent out without proper preparation, background knowledge or speaking the official language. a mandatory advance briefing is therefore a minimum requirement. closer cooperation between the different observation teams is also necessary. too often, observers arrive too late and leave too early. much manipulation is done in advance by tampering with voter registration or banning opposition meetings. sometimes the process is derailed afterwards, when a head of state refuses to accept the result, as in togo. a small core of long-term observers can provide a solution here. of course, all this only makes sense if the reports of the observers are actually followed up. unfortunately, in the past too often economic or strategic interests have prevailed over the interests of democracy and human rights. let us hope that the commission's note and mr fava's excellent report will be followed up and lead to a more coherent and consistent approach to undemocratic regimes.
mr president, i think we can be satisfied - at any rate my group is satisfied - that this debate is taking place at an important time, not only when a trial is taking place which is mobilizing the international press, a trial which is currently suspended in south africa, but also because it is of symbolic value: it is a question of whether the poor have a priority right to access to medicines which can alleviate their suffering or prevent their death. this is a major problem and i am doubly pleased that the main groups in this parliament have agreed on a common text with a strong message. the first message is to the pharmaceutical industry to withdraw its complaint against south africa, which is important, and the second message is that we want a clear interpretation of the trips agreement on intellectual property, or even a revision of the agreement. it is certainly welcome that the european commission, through mr lamy, is taking this approach. i therefore wish to join the previous speakers. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of aids, and i am sure that the commission will be able to take action to combat it. this is particularly the case in india. this should make us all very aware of the need for a system of intellectual property protection in a field like this, where it is vital that people have access to medicines. it seems to me that the debate will be revived by this trial, and will be doubly revived by the interesting position taken by the european parliament.

- mr president, today in this debate on current affairs we are discussing one case of parental abduction, namely that of the limet children who have not visited their father for many years, despite court decisions and diplomatic steps. the reason why this matter is on the list of urgent matters today is that there is a chance that it can be unblocked in the short term thanks to the efforts of the belgian government and the commitments of president arap moi of kenya, and it only needs a little push from this european parliament to get it back on track. but the fact that we are discussing this case here today should not make us forget that the parental kidnapping of the limet children is just the tip of the iceberg. several of us have been involved in parental kidnapping cases, sometimes even as a mediator for a country. i myself am aware of many dozens of cases of parents in belgium whose children have been abducted to tunisia, morocco, lebanon, even america, and also in the european union many children are denied the right to one of their parents. in a recent report i read that in 1999 alone over 1,800 children were the subject of hague convention proceedings. and that is only the case for the 47 countries which have ratified the convention, not even the cases under consideration under the luxembourg convention. all these children have a right to both parents and the european parliament has ensured that this is clearly enshrined in the charter of fundamental rights of the european union proclaimed in nice, even more clearly than in the new york convention. and that is another reason to ensure that this charter is incorporated into our treaty. i hope that the adoption of this resolution will help to guarantee the right of the limet children to both parents, but i am equally convinced that the european parliament, and i hope the commission, will have to take many more initiatives to restore the rights of all those other children and their parents. i am convinced that there is the political will in this house to do so.
- mr president, on behalf of my colleague mr ries, i would like to stress the importance of this resolution. this resolution, although it deals with only one issue, reflects the problems with a european and even an international dimension. these are facts which unfortunately are becoming more and more frequent and which are a violation of international conventions. it is about the destruction of a parent's life, of children and of an environment. for more than two years mr limet has been fighting for his children, who were taken into his care and abducted by his ex-wife to kenya. the mother of the children was recently sentenced to one year's imprisonment without parole for kidnapping, but despite the legal proceedings and the intervention of both the belgian and european political authorities, in particular mr prodi, mr limet's chance of seeing his children again remains virtually nil. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we have seen with horror the images of the unprecedentedly brutal killings taking place in kalimantan. this is clearly a case of ethnic tensions that have gotten out of hand, as they exist in various places in indonesia: in timor, atjeh, the moluccas and irian jaya. in west kalimantan, these riots broke out in 1997 and 1999, and everywhere they are actually the result of the 1960 transmigration program that was imposed. but under the expert control of the dictatorship, all these tensions that used to remain hidden under the skin are now suddenly erupting with violence. i think the international community must be very vigilant and must make sure that it is ready to give the legitimate government the help it needs, because it is poor against poor and it is, after all, a problem of poverty.

- mr president, since the end of the cold war, the proliferation of small arms has become a global problem; we are talking about a phenomenon that is fuelling both street crime in our country and civil wars in the third world. the un conference is therefore late rather than early. i believe that the european union should take the lead in new york. the european code of conduct of 1998 can serve as a model for a global agreement on arms exports and transfers. that code has proven useful, but it has two major flaws. first of all, it is not legally binding. europe must insist that an international agreement be made binding. secondly, there is no provision for checking the end-user of exported arms. in africa, for example, togo and burkina are officially considered to be the end users of arms from bulgaria, when in reality they are being funneled to liberia and to the rebels in sierra leone. europe must advocate stricter controls and sanctions for such transfer countries. the proliferation of small arms is not, however, a problem confined to the third world. gun ownership is also on the rise in europe and north america. half of all american households have a firearm. while these are often legal weapons, they are equally damaging, as was seen in california last week. the un conference must therefore also dare to tackle the problem of legal small arms and europe must dare to advocate a total ban on advertising for firearms. president, the un member states are faced with a difficult dilemma: the european union must be able to take action to combat terrorism and to prevent it from spreading. we are counting heavily on the swedish presidency to steer the preparatory meeting in the right direction and on the belgian presidency to make the right choices at the conference.
- the president, commissioner, colleagues, it is to be feared. a country like mozambique, which has already suffered so much misfortune, is now being hit by severe flooding again. it is clear that the emergency aid is not arriving in time, that many people cannot be evacuated in time and, moreover, one has to ask how such a poor country can overcome this disaster. a few years ago we could have been happy with the rapid economic recovery in some parts of mozambique, but the interior is so terribly poor - the people have nothing at all - that i find it a shame that some countries have still not forgiven mozambique's debts. i think europe should take the lead in this. this country doesn't have to pay its debts. it is already paying such a heavy toll on nature that the utmost solidarity is needed here.

- madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i think there are positive elements in the statements by the president of the council and the president of the commission, particularly when mr persson spoke firmly about the position of the united states on the kyoto protocol and when mr prodi recalled the real issue of the lisbon process, namely an integrated strategy for the economic, social and environmental sectors. however, for the first time in this parliament, the plenary assembly is presented with a joint resolution signed by the socialist party, the greens and the european united left. the significance of this resolution is a clear political signal to both the council and the commission. it is based on a finding, mr persson and mr prodi: the european economy is indeed competitive, but it is neither sustainable nor sufficiently solidary at the moment. this is not an a priori statement: many indicators are in the red, whether in the field of sustainable development, transport, energy - not to mention the common agricultural policy - or in the social sector, where the majority of new jobs created are precarious and where the share of wages in value added is insufficient. the last report of the european environment agency is sufficient in this respect. we must therefore change the direction of development, so that it is more sustainable and more inclusive. this is the real challenge of the 21st century and i should like to make a few suggestions in this connection. mr bolkestein, what are we doing about corporate tax and the primarolo group? us with indicators to ensure that, through open coordination, we not only create more jobs but also better jobs. the quality of the work, ladies and gentlemen, is not just a matter of indicators and open coordination. it is also a matter of legislation. we therefore expect the commission and the council to make a further effort to propose a revised directive on european works councils and to work urgently on the directive on information and consultation in the context of the restructuring we are now experiencing. that too is a guarantee of the quality of the work, and we are counting on the swedish and belgian presidencies to do that.
in writing. - i welcome the fact that the communication implements the process of declaration 22 annexed to the treaty of amsterdam, which states that the needs of disabled people will be taken into account in the development of measures to establish the single market. it refers to the 1996 communication on equal opportunities for disabled people recognising their rights rather than seeing them as people in need of charitable assistance. this is a fundamental change in attitude which should be emphasized. this initiative aims to fill the gaps in the strategy for mainstreaming the rights of disabled people in community policies in the areas of transport, access to buildings and public spaces, the information society, research and development and consumer policy. the concept of disabled person includes all pathologies, whether physical, mental or sensory. this is another fundamental change of importance. the lack of statistics on this has been noted since 1998. to remedy this, eurostat will provide three studies: a new module of the labour force survey for march 2003, the publication, in early 2001, of indicators serving as the basis for the analysis of the level of participation of disabled people in the labour market, cultural life, etc.; the extension of the scope of the community household panel survey on the daily obstacles perceived in each household. the report usefully points out that the first barriers to disabled people arise mainly during childhood. following the luxembourg charter on education for all, the first place in education must be left to parents, so that they are given the means of making a genuine choice, in respect of their family values and traditions. the commission has already done so. the commission's proposed regulation represents a significant simplification compared with the existing arrangements. the commission considers that the aid granted to the airport operator is compatible with the internal market within the meaning of article 107 (3) (c) of the treaty. on the other hand, rail, inland waterway and combined transport should be compensated for the uncovered costs of competing road transport in order to allow specific concerns such as environmental friendliness and regional potential to be taken into account in transport policy. an approach which is aimed solely at avoiding possible distortions of competition and which does not address environmental, social and regional aspects is not acceptable. the rapporteur, whom i thank for his excellent work, and the committee have set out further important premises which my group supports, such as the inclusion of aid for short sea shipping, including combined transport operations in seaports; independence of the aid from the public or private management of the undertakings; minimising administrative burden by introducing lower thresholds where no commission verification or notification is required; extension of the period of the aid and possibility of extending the aid scheme. the implementation of this regulation opens up another possibility to achieve the shift towards environmentally friendly modes of transport.
- mr president, madam vice-president, i have to say that i am very pleased with the way the commission has handled this matter. i should also like to thank the rapporteur for his report. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. a system of aid for transport infrastructure and use was important because it will increase transparency, and in addition to that we can also expect more efficiency and environmentally friendly and safe transport through this directive, if this is done in small steps. although this directive does not specifically address a particular mode of transport, we do expect that road transport can be relieved. this can only be done if there is no false competition between the different means of transport. i think this regulation is setting new standards and the committee has also proposed various amendments which go in two directions. firstly, as mr jarzembowski has already said, we think it is important to define the concept of external costs more precisely. it cannot be interpreted differently from country to country or region to region, because that would be a real distortion. secondly, we also believe that distortions of competition should be avoided by giving equal consideration to the modes of transport. if a state invests in transport infrastructure which should be equally accessible to all users, then no distinction should be made between these modes of transport. whether this aid is now intended for rail, road or inland port, the aid must be measured against the criterion of accessibility of the infrastructure to users in order to guarantee fair competition. we cannot see why, according to this philosophy, multimodal platforms or inland ports can be de facto subject to notification. we expect simpler administrative procedures and, accordingly, equal treatment for all modes of transport.

- the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the labelling, presentation and advertising of foodstuffs for sale to the ultimate consumer; people do not ask me why we are voting on this directive, many people ask me to do this as quickly and as well as possible. i myself come from a country where there are already some rules on this matter. i would like to express my gratitude to the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done and the good improvements he has made to the commission's proposal. the most important of these - he himself has mentioned it - is the obligation to collect all electrical and electronic equipment and to set higher and more stringent collection targets for 2006. the essential objective of this directive is set out in article 1. first prevention, then reuse, then recycling and finally energy recovery. it is this hierarchy that is not respected in the commission proposal, because prevention is being moved to another directive and the reuse of whole appliances is not being recognised. the rapporteur has also referred to a number of cases of energy recovery rather than recycling. the best waste prevention, my fellow citizens, is to change the ecodesign of these products. that is why the instrument of individual producer responsibility, financial responsibility, is so essential. the reuse of whole appliances is also important, not only from an environmental point of view, but also from a social point of view. social, because it increases employment opportunities for the low-skilled, but also because it makes products more accessible at a lower price. it is also clear that this directive must provide guarantees that waste is not exported to third countries except for reuse, and only if it can be guaranteed that the same high objectives and standards are respected in those third countries. the ban on dangerous substances is the best preventive measure we can take. in addition, this is also important for the health of the workers. in fact, the directive on the restriction of the use of certain hazardous substances in electrical and electronic equipment is part of the preventive objective set out in article 1 of the directive on waste electrical and electronic equipment. that is why i was so in favour of merging the two directives. we have also known for a long time about the problem of heavy metals. for most of them, alternatives are available and for some of them the commission provides for exceptions. i do not think it is parliament's place to make exceptions quickly, quickly and within a few days. it also has the disadvantage that it is sometimes too much inspired by lobbies. let the commission do its job and come out in two years with a modified version of the heavy metal exemptions. finally, i would like to mention the debate on brominated flame retardants. it's become a debate between believers, like me, who are convinced of the environmental and health impacts, and the non-believers. what i think is important is that this discussion has led many to a greater awareness of the harmfulness of these products. a ban in 2006 is not possible. the amendment tabled by my group calls for producers to demonstrate by 2003 that these products are harmless, and i hope that this can be achieved.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. mr president, i should like to stress four amendments to the original proposal submitted by the european parliament, with the support of both the legal affairs committee, which was unanimous, and the environment committee. the first of these amendments, amendment no 3, relates to one of the major innovations contained in the commission's proposal, namely the management of europe's water resources on the basis of the water catchment areas of the rivers and streams that irrigate the territory of the union. in this connection, parliament urged the commission to take account of the fact that many rivers and streams, whether in southern europe, the tagus, in the centre, the rhine, or in the north, the oder, are border rivers which concern either several member states or member states, but also coastal states other than member states, and in particular the candidate countries. parliament considered it essential that these non-member coastal states be involved in the management procedure now and without waiting for eventual but inevitably future accession. a second amendment, amendment no 8, calls on the commission to promote the synchronisation of research and conclusions drawn from the two major pollution abatement mechanisms already in place. the first, ospar, is focused on seawater but covers a significant part of inland waters. the second, the commps procedure, starts from inland waters but also covers a significant part of maritime waters. in these circumstances it is easy to understand that there will be chaos if the commission does not ensure the requested timing. finally, the third amendment, amendment no 11, expresses the wish that the commission, while referring primarily to the commps procedure, should not exclude the use of techniques for the assessment of the harmfulness of certain substances already developed or even used in other pollution control measures. for example, mechanisms which have proved successful in systems developed for air pollution must be able to be used or transferred to water pollution. my last remark is intended to underline the exceptional overall importance of the texts submitted to parliament for a vote. one of the reasons for europe's overall exemplary development, for the balanced nature of this development and for the quality of culture which has always accompanied the economic development of our continent is precisely that europe has always had inexhaustible resources, because they are infinitely renewable, of the precious liquid to which we owe our lives. both the quality and quantity of water are now being threatened by the senseless and rampant pollution that has been practically unchecked over the past few centuries. no state, no region can avoid taking action in this area.

as we unfortunately know it on the road - almost only large transport companies have a chance of surviving in the medium term. in any case, it is becoming increasingly difficult for small transport companies, because competition is very strong and partly at the expense of safety, at the expense of driving and rest periods, but also at the expense of proper working conditions. that is what this proposal is partly addressing and partly resolving. the introduction of the driver's licence is a first answer, but only but at least a partial solution. we must not forget that many hauliers from neighbouring third countries benefit from the situation and from certain authorisations, such as cemt licences or bilateral transport permits, to work with low social and wage standards. we are therefore called upon to look beyond this report to this subject, if we are to be honest about transport, working conditions and especially road safety. the rapporteur has proposed that the scope of this regulation be limited to drivers from third countries. the commission - and i think this is very good - was of the opinion that this should be extended to all drivers. the amendments we are proposing are a healthy compromise, because we want to include third countries in the first phase and all drivers in the second phase, because we see this as a general measure which will also be useful for all drivers. this also makes it possible to spread the administrative burden over two levels. i know personally, as a resident of a border community, that there is also a lot to be controlled in this area at the intra-european level. and in the future, when we have the new tachograph and the map, we could also introduce an integrated control system that could take into account both social and safety aspects. we therefore propose to work in these two phases, and we also hope that countries will realise, as with any regulation, that any regulation is only efficient if the controls are efficient.

mr president, for years the european parliament has been calling for community initiatives on pensions and we therefore welcome the principles agreed in the council and the work prepared by the commission. the aim is to use the texts before us to promote convergence between the systems by means of cooperation and coordination between the member states. it's about two angles. on the one hand, there are a number of common challenges, such as the decline and ageing of the population or the ratio between young and old, the changing degree of dependency or the ratio between active and inactive, the limitations of budgetary resources, technical possibilities in health care, etc. on the other hand, there are also a number of crossborder implications: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social security system is not disrupted by the introduction of the single market, the mobility of persons or the opportunity to work and retire in another member state, the mobility of capital or the opportunity to save and retire in another member state, the supplementary pension funds which, unlike the compulsory pension schemes, appeal to people in several member states and even in other countries. for all these reasons, systems need to be made sustainable in order to cope with common challenges and cross-border complexities. the report by the committee on employment and social affairs shows how the european parliament and the european people's party are thinking about the convergence of pension systems. it is not for the european union to dictate to the member states how pensions should be organised and financed. member states themselves choose the appropriate relationship between compulsory schemes and supplementary schemes, between a conversion scheme and capitalisation, between contributions and benefits or between subsistence or welfare guarantees. but on the one hand, solutions must be found to the difficulties which hamper the free movement of pensions and pensioners and, on the other hand, efforts must be made to ensure that the efficiency and effectiveness of the systems are monitored by means of budgetary resources which serve social objectives and not vice versa. the committee on employment and social affairs and the epp also believe that the combination of cooperation between member states and coordination between systems is the appropriate way to secure pensions and reassure pensioners, and that this should be made clear at the gothenburg european council. i think that the luxembourg summit on employment a few years ago, where the open method of coordination was actually introduced in practice, has shown a very good way forward, but of course a link must be made between pensions and employment. as long as europe and the various member states do not succeed in solving the problems of employing people over 55 and do not put more pressure on them, create more opportunities and almost impose more obligations, the problem of pensions will not be solved. it is essential to establish a link between the two. let this be one of the guidelines in the context of coordination and the indicators that will be drawn up.
- mr president, first of all, on behalf of my group, i should like to thank commissioner diamantopoulou very warmly for her good work in this communication which gives us the opportunity to discuss pensions in a different way and also to congratulate my colleague mr cercas alonso on his excellent report. i hope, mr president-in-office, that our opinion, which we shall be voting on tomorrow, will inspire the gothenburg summit and, in particular, will encourage the council to involve the european parliament in a more structured way in the future in the discussion on pensions under the open method of coordination. i am grateful to the swedish presidency for the work that has already been done in this area. i hope very much that göteborg can give a clear mandate to the belgian presidency to formulate common social objectives in the context of european cooperation on pensions. as mr smet said earlier, it is of course the member states who decide what pension scheme they want, how they organise it, how they guarantee its financial viability. but the fundamental objective of maintaining and improving the quality of pension systems in europe for all citizens is an objective which we must all share. it is clear that the dynamics of economic and monetary integration have already put the pension issue on the european agenda, albeit very unilaterally, namely through the debate on the financiability of pension systems. we believe that the essential point should never be lost sight of in the discussion of pension systems, namely the modernization of pension systems so that we can guarantee the present and future generations of pensioners a decent pension and participation in social life. that debate is also about solidarity and also about equality between men and women. we believe that a number of principles are essential. i would like to name four. first, pension systems must be able to combat poverty in an efficient way. it is unacceptable that pensions, especially those for women, are still below the poverty line in the european union. i would therefore dare to invite the commission to re-examine this pension dossier on minimum benefits on the basis of the 1992 recommendation. secondly, proper pensions are obviously more than just a living wage. it's about ensuring a certain standard of living. it is therefore important that the link between general welfare growth and pensions be maintained. thirdly, the second pillar of the pension must never replace the first. because it is the first pension pillar that provides the fundamental guarantee that everyone will have access to a proper pension. for my group, the question is no longer whether we are for or against the second pension pillar, but how we ensure that solidarity is organised even within the second pension pillar so that supplementary pensions do not remain a privilege for a small group. of course, i could also discuss, mr president, the need to increase the level of activity, but i will not do that. let me be clear about one thing: for my group, this does not mean a general increase in the mandatory retirement age, but measures to encourage people to stay longer at work and to encourage companies not to throw old workers out on the streets. i hope that göteborg will be able to translate these principles into common objectives, mr president-in-office.
the council. the reform of the sugar market organisation has been discussed in the council since october last year. in this discussion, the council has taken note of certain criticisms made by the court of auditors. as the house is aware, the european parliament delivered its opinion on this matter on 13 march 2001. looking at this opinion, i find that it does not generally follow the line proposed by the honourable member. i also note that the commission has indicated that it may consider accepting an extension of the current sugar regime beyond 1 july 2003, provided that all other parts of its reform proposal are accepted by the council. i also note that there is a division of opinion among the member states between those who are in favour of reform and those who are against reform. the council will continue its consideration of the matter at its may meeting and will take into account all relevant contributions, including those made in the question now before the council. the council also adopted a regulation on 'everything but arms' on 26 february 2001. this regulation extends the duty-free regime without quantitative limitation to all products, except arms and ammunition, originating in a large number of the least developed countries. as regards sugar, full liberalisation of trade will apply from 1 july 2009.
- i understand the caution with which the council must respond, given the lack of unanimity among the countries of the european union, but it is a shame. in fact, we are subsidizing the sugar industry - last year it was over 800 million euros - as if there were not enough sugar in the world and as if we were waiting for these subsidies to keep our farmers going. i really think that the policy we are pursuing towards the developing countries is and remains hypocritical. a fine initiative like 'everything but arms' is in fact tarnished by the fact that we maintain our hypocritical attitude and prefer to continue to carry out those things which interest us rather than support the sugar producers who have traditionally belonged to the cabinets of our 19th century industrialists. i am sure that we have made an exception once again. i hope that the council will continue to press for unanimity on reforms.
