mr president, i have little time, so i have little opportunity to stress the importance of european party formation. but if you say that parties are necessary, you must also know that they provide financial resources, and until now they have done so in an unorthodox way. it had to be done in the twilight zone. there must be a firm, serious foundation. there must be transparency and control. that is why our group is in principle in favour of not allowing corporate donations to political parties in order to guarantee the independence of parliament as an institution and of individual members. every country has had its scandals and we know that it is necessary to act very strictly and with control. if there are donations, it must be very clear and transparent where they come from and where the money goes. democratic party formation should be possible without too high a threshold. we therefore support the commission's proposal from the outset to use five countries as a standard so that we do not intimidate forces, if they are at least democratic forces. there must also be sufficient resources for all recognised parties to be able to function as a party. the fixed costs - and this can be objectively verified - must be covered before the resources are distributed proportionately. finally, we must be very aware that the citizens are watching the system very closely. we ourselves must ensure that the basic democratic rights are respected, because we cannot subsidize parties in order to undermine democracy.
- mr president, commissioner, bosnia-herzegovina is actually the touchstone of our dream of stability and peace. the international community has so far made efforts to support the peacekeeping forces in those countries. of course, there are also counter forces, and those counter forces create victims. in addition to the fact that there are still attempts on various sides to put their own great equality first and to threaten bosnia-herzegovina as a unity, there are of course also the religious conflicts. conflicts that have to do with symbols and everyone who is involved in politics knows how important symbols are. that is certainly true in a country torn apart by war. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we cooperate decisively with all the international and, in particular, all the local forces which want peace on the ground. it is therefore also of great importance that we can guarantee that our presence there will not be short-lived, that we will continue to live up to our commitment and that we can also persuade the united states, which with a new bush administration is a threat to that lasting stability, to make a lasting commitment to peace in bosnia-herzegovina.
- mr president, commissioner, until recently we had to talk about the war between ethiopia and eritrea. fortunately, that is over and this proud and beautiful ethiopian people can once again take charge of their own destiny. unfortunately, we have to find that human rights are being violated, that there are torture practices, that people are being arbitrarily detained in prisons, that students are arguing in vain for academic freedom. this hurts. as an international community, we have concluded a partnership agreement. we will sign it as the cotonou agreement. we are trying to establish good relations through dialogue. this is particularly true of ethiopia, which is always on the list of countries eligible for emergency aid. i hope that we can finally turn over a new leaf with ethiopia, and that ethiopia will be prepared - and i think we must insist on this, commissioner - to release the persons currently imprisoned because they are human rights activists, namely mr woldemariam, mr nega and mr lidetu ayalew, to allow the students to return to their campuses in full freedom and to respect fundamental freedoms.
- mr president, few african leaders have been able to rely on as much international goodwill as the ethiopian leader meles zenawi when he took office. the end of the military dictatorship, the peaceful separation of eritrea and the formation of a government of national unity made the country a model of successful transition from dictatorship to democracy. unfortunately, that image has been brutally shattered. the bloody border war with eritrea, the government's inability to prevent a new famine and the repression of recent weeks have seriously damaged ethiopia's image. it is true that unemployed young people used the student protests earlier this month to carry out looting, but that in no way explains the excessive harshness of the police forces that has left at least 30 dead. nor does it account for the wave of repression in which students, journalists, human rights activists and dissidents were arrested and accused of inciting violence. ethiopia is facing two fundamental problems: firstly, the thin ethnic base of the government and secondly, poverty. the first is that there is an urgent need for an inter-ethiopian dialogue in which all political and ethnic groups are represented, and europe can play a very important role in this. the second is that the ethiopian government should unconditionally support the peace agreement with eritrea so that the funds needed for development are no longer diverted to defence expenditure and arms.
- mr president, this is not the first time that ngos and human rights organizations have sounded the alarm about the human rights situation in cameroon. they speak of disappearances, extrajudicial executions, torture, mass graves and overcrowded prisons. the special unit for combating banditry in particular is alleged to be engaged in systematic violations of human rights. the un committee against torture rightly called for the rapid dismantling of this unit. but of course it's not enough. there is a huge problem of lawlessness in cameroon. it is positive that the president, mr biya, has set up a commission of inquiry which has led to the arrest of some suspected officers. but to restore confidence in the rule of law, more is needed, such as an independent investigation into the disappearances and the mass graves in particular, and above all the punishment of those responsible. europe could help by providing resources and expertise to the legal apparatus in cameroon. without wishing to provide an alibi for the leaders of cameroon, i would like to draw attention to one of the fundamental problems of cameroon and indeed of large parts of africa, namely the dramatic fall in the price of raw materials. oxfam has today presented a report which assumes that the fall in the prices of these commodities costs the developing countries more than the total amount they receive in development aid. i would therefore urge the council and the commission to take the recommendation of the oxfam report seriously and perhaps we can have a debate on this in the near future. it is hopeful that the belgian presidency has announced today that it wants to make africa one of its priorities, and we welcome that and we will cooperate wholeheartedly in that.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, when 43 children are found on board a ship which takes them away from home to work on plantations in another country, we are rightly shocked. we thought slavery had been abolished, but slavery still exists, and the most shocking form of it is, of course, the trade in children and the trade in child slaves. children are shipped off to work on plantations. children are abducted to be trained as soldiers, to carry weapons, and to become killing machines or to perform sexual services. children are orphaned prematurely when their parents succumb to aids and are then taken in as house slaves by supposedly sympathetic caretakers. in short, children are often slaves. yet africa does not have the tradition of a continent where the sale of children was the custom. extreme poverty is the cause of this child slavery and child labour which is contrary to the convention on the rights of the child. i therefore urge, commissioner, as the resolution states, that the networks which set up child trafficking be tackled and dismantled, that there be reintegration programmes for rescued or escaped children, but also for former child soldiers and street children, and above all that we help to ensure that the products of africa are sold at a fair price, so that parents can work and their children can go to school properly.
- mr. president, according to unicef, 200,000 children are trafficked each year in west and central africa. poor families who are in debt lend their child to a creditor as collateral or to earn back the amount. but because they cannot get out of debt, the children are often sold and taken to other countries. there they are used as domestic helpers, unpaid laborers on cocoa or cotton plantations, or in the sex industry. child traffickers are rarely caught or punished. the discovery of the nigerian ship etireno in the port of cotonou may be just the tip of the iceberg. the international labour organization estimates that there are 250 million child slaves worldwide. it is unacceptable that practices such as child trafficking and slavery continue to exist in the 21st century and even spread. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the european economic area. the legal apparatus in the country should be given much more resources and be better equipped to combat this kind of abuse, and multinational companies should impose on themselves the moral code not to exploit children for commercial purposes. at the same time, we must dare to tackle the underlying causes. poverty and unemployment. let us hope that the promise of debt relief made this week at the conference of the least developed countries in brussels will be upheld and that our markets will finally be opened to agricultural products from these countries so that we can give these people something to sell other than their children.

and we must be clear on this point - we believe that the new framework programme must play a role as a tool for structuring the european area of brussels. past evaluations of previous programmes show that they are useful because they create contacts at european level, but do not have a sufficient structuring effect. that is quite clear. that is why we need new instruments. i understand the apprehension whenever we talk about "new". adapting to change is difficult and that's normal. so it is a very understandable mechanism and i understand some apprehensions, hence the need to explain it well. i would say, however, that calls for proposals remain relevant in the so-called 'eighth priority', which joins the support for emerging policies and policies. so there's over 1.6 billion euros available for calls for proposals in the traditional sense. so the motion is heard. as regards the general budget, i think that, although it seems to have some of the council's approval, i understand that the swedish presidency's proposal will be to hold a first informal discussion on the budget at the research council on 26 june. i hope our proposal will be taken up. it is a measured proposal, which is also in line with the financial perspectives drawn up in berlin for the various commission policies. so it seems to me that there will be a possibility. finally, the joint research centre. as you know, we have made an effort to focus the joint research centre on the tasks for which it is best suited - that is to say, tasks of joint control, joint testing and scientific expertise in environmental problems, health, nuclear energy in certain respects, and security in relation to the information society. there are tools here, and of course we have a programme at the level of the joint research centre which, in budgetary terms, is certainly not growing but which concentrates the centre's role more. i therefore believe that the joint research centre still has a future if it is given tasks which are useful to the european union as a whole.
mr president, i would like to ask you to give me a few brief comments on the commission's proposal. in the area of diseases, including mad cow disease and the transmission of spongiform encephalopathy, 48 research projects were defined in the previous programme following the onset of the process. so we have a document which brings together all that has been done and at the last council of research ministers it was decided to establish - which was a first - at european level a meeting of all researchers - i am not talking about veterinarians, veterinary schools - researchers, on the problems of mad cow disease, but also on the problems of creuzfelt jacob's disease which is also, at the human level, particularly worrying and interesting. i am pleased to say to the members of this house that we have done a work which has been recognised as unique, which has just taken stock of all the research projects in europe. this is interesting work in itself because good researchers in different countries were surprised to learn that there were others in other countries doing similar research. this document will be available at the research council on 26 june and will be made public. i have already had the opportunity to give a press conference announcing it, showing the outlines, but i think it is very concrete. as regards the question of whether or not to slaughter animals, i think we should refer to my colleagues, mr byrne or mr fischler, since these are policies which are defined either at the level of health and consumer protection or at the level of the common agricultural policy.

- mr president, the central question remains: the european union is not prepared for the next historic enlargement to central and eastern europe. the answer is clearly no! nice is too narrow a step forward. but we still can't throw the baby out with the bath water. the new treaty also contains positive elements, and it would be a grave political mistake to simply wipe this new treaty off the table. it is now time to look ahead, to a wider and at the same time deeper debate on the future of the european union. everything seems to be focused on the belgian presidency and the laeken declaration. but we must not get our hopes up: laeken does not bring a new treaty, it cannot solve the shortcomings that have remained after nice. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. let us hope that at that time both the present member states and the candidate countries will be ready for a thorough deepening of the european integration process.
- mr president, commissioner, the paradox of the european union is that, on the one hand, its environmental legislation has played a pioneering role for many countries in the union, including my own, and will certainly play a pioneering role for the candidate countries. the other part of the paradox is that many of the sectoral policies of the european union are in fact contrary to the criteria of sustainable development, including in particular the agricultural policy, but more generally also the emphasis on economic growth, without sufficient consideration for sustainable development, as has just been pointed out - i would like to draw the attention of the commission and the council to this - in a recent oecd report which is much more critical than usual of commission texts. i think it is urgent, and that is the sense of our support for the two reports of the european parliament and the myller report which is linked to it, that there should be an urgent change of direction. politically, in gothenburg, three requirements. firstly, the european council must endorse the commission's text without diluting the guidelines proposed by the commission. secondly, the environment council, which is to meet one week before gothenburg, must take account of the european parliament's requests for the sixth framework programme. and thirdly, the belgian presidency must be given the task of giving concrete follow-up to the guidelines that will be decided in gothenburg. as regards economic aspects in particular, the ecofin council must change its agenda. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's environmental policy is not distorted by the fact that it is not in line with the principles of the treaty of rome. environmental and social criteria are needed in public procurement; on the question of indicators, it is not acceptable that the shortlist of indicators does not currently contain any environmental criteria. national accounts incorporating environmental data should also be considered. these are some of the points we wish to make. we have to be able to afford it. and we will be attentive, during the belgian presidency, to the barcelona european council being the first european council to really integrate environmental, social and economic strategy.
- mr president, i should like to concentrate in my brief intervention on one important new feature in the directive and in the excellent report by mrs hautala, namely, gender-based harassment. several studies have shown that many hundreds of thousands of workers, mostly women, are affected by this, with all the negative consequences for their health and their careers. i would remind colleagues that the etuc was therefore in favour of collective bargaining to tackle this problem three years ago, but unfortunately the employers' organisation has always refused to do so. that is why it is necessary and useful for the legislator to act now. i am therefore pleased, commissioner, that the proposal considers sexual harassment to be a form of discrimination on grounds of sex. i think the european commission's sanctioning approach is good, but perhaps not enough. the european parliament would therefore do well to ensure that there is also an effective prevention policy, as was also provided for in the code of conduct for the 1991 recommendation on the protection of the dignity of men and women at work. we believe that employers must also take responsibility for ensuring that sexual harassment does not occur in the workplace. in belgium, minister onkelinx recently tabled a draft law requiring each company to draw up a prevention plan and also to appoint a prevention adviser. i think that is a good approach which can certainly be imitated in europe. i therefore hope that this directive, as it stands now with the amendments introduced by our committee, can be adopted under the belgian presidency.

- mr president, if the belgian dioxin crisis, the bse crisis and the foot-and-mouth disease epidemic have taught us anything, it is that there is a need for strict regulation of the trade, storage and processing of animal waste. each year, some 16 million tons of animal waste must be disposed of. since 4 december 2000, there has been a total ban on feeding animal protein to farm animals. the proposal for a regulation we are discussing today sets out how animal waste should be handled in future and how to prevent unwanted products from entering the food chain. it brings order to the sector, creating clearly separated waste streams that must not be mixed under any circumstances. marit paulsen described all the bottlenecks very clearly in her introduction. this regulation is of the utmost importance. if it turns out that the three-categorization, the mandatory separation of waste streams and the prevention of cannibalism in the sector are not or are not sufficiently respected and that the fraud is too great or remains too great, then we can assume that the political preconditions for allowing the use of meat and bone meal in pig and poultry feed have not been met. then there will be legal arguments for maintaining the total ban. i therefore ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to give your massive support to amendment no 64 to article 26, which states that the temporary ban on feeding all types of animal by-products should not be extended until the entry into force of the present regulation, as the commission wants, but until the regulation is fully applied in the member states. we therefore want a report from the member states on the uniform application of this regulation before 1 february 2003 and only after receiving this report can the debate on the lifting of the total ban be launched. we must be sure that the regulation is working, that fraud is virtually excluded, that the sector has learned the necessary lessons from the crisis. i believe, ladies and gentlemen, that this regulation offers opportunities to the sector, it offers a fair chance. i hope the sector will seize this opportunity.

- mr president, we are in favour of this directive. i will not conceal that there has been a whole series of discussions on precise points. the most important point in the report which has been discussed in our group is that it says that the selfemployed should be included. the majority of our group agrees with this, but it does not take away from the fact that the debate is now much more difficult than it was in the first reading, when the commission's position was in line with that of parliament, namely that the selfemployed should be included. but i repeat, the majority of our group is in favour of including the selfemployed. why do people support that? the first reason is that a selfemployed person, like everyone else, has a right to health and safety at work. a second reason is competition. it is irresponsible to allow companies, whether selfemployed or employed, to compete with each other in terms of working time, as is the case in the common position of the council and the commission. the third reason - and this is probably the reason for which the public is rightly most sensitive - is that there are many accidents to be regretted, which very often are due to the fact that people have far too long working hours and there is too little control, and that control is further complicated by the fact that the tachographs are cheated. people are victims, whether they are tourists heading south or motorists being run off the road by a sleepy truck driver. i think it is our duty to settle these matters at european level. that doesn't change the fact that it's still a matter of debate. the epp will not support two points. first, the text as it was considered in the commission states that the worker must be informed of the beginning and duration of the availability of time 12 hours or a certain number of hours in advance and before the end of the previous shift. we are not going to support this because we think that this is actually far too difficult to organise given the unpredictability of traffic flows. then a second point. amendment no 8 would require the employer to charge for the time spent by a non-driving worker alongside the driver of a moving vehicle as working time. we share the commission's view that this should remain available time and not working time.
the rapporteur. - mr president, madam commissioner, there are two sides to this problem. we certainly cannot discourage shipping: it is an environmentally friendly form and it is economically very necessary. but at the same time, we must control the risks that go with it. we need to do a better job of managing traffic, if i may say so. for every accident at sea affects not only those directly involved, the people on the ship, but also everyone who lives on the sea and lives from the sea. after the erika accident, we worked fast. however, new accidents have continued to put us under pressure and we must take action. i would like to thank the commission for the good cooperation and the colleagues from the transport committee for allowing this committee to submit a proposal to you unanimously. firstly, we must improve the monitoring of shipping traffic, which means increasing the powers of the coastal and port states and imposing a modest restriction on the right of free passage. we must not forget that the erika sank 70 kilometres off the coast of brittany, which is far beyond the territorial waters. secondly, we must introduce an automatic reporting requirement via transponders. i have elaborated the commission's proposal on this point in a little more detail. i think it is also necessary that the data which then automatically enters should also be automatically exchanged between the different member states. that is far too little at the moment. we have to figure that out. the range of the transponder, which is now 30 nautical miles, should be increased as soon as possible and we should move to what is known in english as longrange. thirdly: the black box. that is a difficult point which was already raised in mr watts' report, which has indeed come to a standstill on this point. i think that we as a parliament must continue to put pressure on the imo and therefore we must not deviate from our timetable. fourthly: the commission has proposed a ban on departure in bad weather. i admit that bad weather played a major role in the accidents that occurred, but we in the transport committee are not in favour of the general measure proposed by the commission. sometimes it is safer for ships to be out of harbour than in harbour in bad weather. every port or ship is specific. we must also remember that the captain is the boss of his ship and that we must respect that. that is why we have specified that recommendation for a grounding or have changed it to a recommendation that must receive a reasoned reply from the captain. we ask that station tugs be ready in case of bad weather, so that in case of emergency they can act immediately so that we do not lose time if something happens. there are examples of this in france and the netherlands. environmental risks should not be the only reason for action, but - as other members have also pointed out - risks to people, crew and passengers, among others, are also reasons for action. i've added to that the bunkering at sea, which is also a risky operation. it's dangerous, you know. for example, in april of this year there was a collision off the coast of belgium between a bunker ship that was refuelling and a ship that was being refuelled. a relatively small amount of oil, 10,000 litres, was spilled into the sea, polluting the coastal area at middelkerke, nieuwpoort, etc., which is a very damaging thing. statistics show that the number of bunker accidents is increasing tremendously and rapidly. so we have to do something about that. limiting bunkering and even banning it in bad weather is one of the options. we added that. fifthly: ports of escape. the erika accident was a problem. the erika was looking for an airfield and found none. at the beginning of this year the petrol tanker castor was stranded for six weeks in the mediterranean because it could not find a port to call at. it is understandable, because a port may suffer damage in such a case and it is not certain that it will be compensated. so there must be a real system of safe havens and safe anchorages where the damage can be contained. we think that's a good idea, but we've been working on it a little bit. we must ensure that the ships that call at our ports and sail along our coasts are insured, that is solvent, that is able to pay for the damage they cause, and we must also ensure that there are facilities to intercept ships, repair them, etc. all in all, commissioner, i think we have a good proposal here, that it will be a good directive. we need to get it approved as soon as possible. as there are a number of issues that can evolve very quickly, we have added an evaluation article. the proposal now goes to the council. i hope that the swedish presidency will be able to settle the matter at the end of the month. i am afraid, however, that the proposal will be passed on to the belgian presidency. that should then be a priority and ensure that it is settled by the end of the year at least. however, there are still a number of matters which we have to deal with, even if we have adopted all the erika guidelines. i think, for example, that we should take additional measures to build up routing systems further, for example in the baltic sea. it is necessary to extend this system and also to invest in people, people on board, people on shore, people who carry out the checks, people to man the ships. to emphasize this, we have also included it in the directive, because a law alone is not enough. the real security work is done at sea.

- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, after the sinister fate of afghan women and the bamiyan buddha statues, the taliban are taking another step forward in their unscrupulous religious fanaticism. the sikh and hindu minorities are required to wear a yellow badge on their clothing. human rights watch today reports the latest atrocity in this never ending story: 60 civilians were imprisoned and official, residential and commercial buildings destroyed. when the regime in kabul took power, it was going to be recognized based on three criteria: the european union is committed to the elimination of drug trafficking, the fight against terrorism and respect for human rights. the taliban have only achieved notorious success in reducing opium production. they are therefore convinced that they will never gain international recognition and instead choose the mass repression, or rather the collective murder, of their people. the first consequence of the isolationist policy is that all negotiations have become impossible and therefore the only consequence of the sanctions is that a weary people are punished, who can only flee to camps in the hope of reaching europe. the question is no longer whether the taliban are violating human rights, but what conclusions should be drawn from this. our group therefore tabled two additional amendments. if the taliban also capture panchi, they will see themselves as big conquerors of the west and the russians, and will completely renounce any compromise with the international community. this would lead to the displacement of the ngos, which are the only remaining source of information. europe could propose lifting sanctions and at the same time increasing diplomatic pressure on pakistan. this would slow down the taliban's advance and, above all, bring them back to the negotiating table. but this can only be done by investing in the ngos that are working there and trying to improve the situation of women, children and men.

- mr president, i should like to thank the rapporteur because i have learned something from her report, and i always find that very pleasing. the report also points to the need to amend the directives. it is true that we must control swine fever, but methods of eradication which require mass slaughter are no longer accepted by public opinion. that's the problem. i'm from flanders myself. that's a very densely populated country, but there are still more pigs than people. that means that we ourselves are faced with this problem very strongly. i therefore agree that the fight against swine fever is important, but at the same time i would point out that this animal disease, unlike other animal diseases, does not pose a danger to humans. i note that the eradication of disease is one thing, but it is difficult to distinguish it from the eradication of economic reasons, for example because stables become overcrowded or because animals become too heavy for sale. that is why it is always a little hypocritical to contemplate mass slaughter, because it is not so clear whether it is done for the prevention of disease or for economic reasons. i have to be honest, i don't have a whole lot of confidence in the industry in that respect. the report has convinced me that there has been a remarkable development in the marker vaccine. so in my view it can easily be declared ready for use so that it would be used on a much wider scale than just in emergencies. if it is not reliable, for example because there is still a possibility of transmission via the placenta, then it seems to me logical that it cannot be used in testimony but that it can be used in other cases. if, as the report says, the diagnostic method is still not reliable, then i think that everything must be done to develop good diagnostic methods so that a distinction can be made between vaccinated animals on the one hand and animals that have developed antibodies because they have had the disease or have been in contact with the disease in some other way. i therefore believe that the eradication method as a method of controlling this animal disease should be abandoned and that we should concentrate on the development not only of the merck vaccine, because it already exists, but of good diagnostic methods and on the refinement of the vaccine so that it can also be used for sows. in any case, the directives should provide for a system which makes mass slaughter unnecessary. i agree with one of the commission's conclusions, namely that these mass slaughters are actually a stain on the agricultural policy and the health policy that we as a european community wish to pursue.

- madam president, out of the 15 member states, 12 have introduced a right of succession on the resale of works of art, but one of these 12 does not apply it, while three states have not yet used this technique. since the existing legislation differed greatly not only in its precise purpose but also in the rates applicable and the persons to whom they were applied, this constituted a gap in the internal market. the compromise reached by parliament, the council and the commission is intended to fill this gap and to put an end to the distortion of competition which the community is currently suffering from. furthermore, the right of succession constitutes a fair return for the artists or their beneficiaries, in so far as it compensates for the fact that the laws of the art market mean that a work of art usually only acquires value over time, whereas an artist cannot wait until the twilight of his life to be able to make a living from his art. finally, the royalty is a form of tax on financial speculation in the field of art. a tax which the art market can easily bear, as is shown by the introduction, by directive 94/5, of a special vat system for works of art, a special system which did not, on the contrary, prevent sales from increasing, even in those states which did not have a vat system. the rapporteur has said what he had to say about the compromise reached, which i personally endorsed. in this compromise, the european parliament has achieved two main things: firstly, the lowering of the threshold for the application of the right of succession to € 3000, which is still a lot, but could be revised downwards if the commission deemed it necessary, and it becomes more acceptable insofar as the possibility of a right of succession is kept below the european threshold; - secondly, the rejection of the extravagant time limit of 15 years, which was adopted by the council of ministers in the text adopted at the end of the first reading and which is now reduced to a maximum of six years. this precedent was set at a particularly unfortunate time, at the very moment when the community is preparing to enlarge to include new members. the external art market has not been mistaken about the importance of the proposal submitted to parliament today, so there is no good reason to reject it, and that is why our group will vote for it.

mr president, commissioner, the vote in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, which has divided the committee and has led to the reintroduction of 133 amendments in the plenary, is a good example of the differences in approach to this draft directive. we in our group interpret it as follows: in fact, the main purpose of this directive on occupational pension funds is to remove all the conditions which restrict financial institutions, particularly in the choice of financial assets, with the exception of general prudential rules, so-called qualitative rules, beyond which the principle of mutual recognition will apply in full. we are faced here with a well-known situation in the completion of the internal market. as for the degree of harmonization or integration which is desirable, or even liberalization and openness, but where what fritz scharps calls positive integration is lagging behind the objective of liberalization, from this point of view i would simply like to mention three points which are particularly important to us: - the problem of risk cover as such, the so-called biometric risks: we will support amendment no 108, tabled by our colleague mr herzog; - the problem of tax competition (amendment no 117), an example which shows that we are making more progress, and much faster, towards the integration of markets without prior tax harmonization: this is a point of view which we do not support in order to support the question of capital risk cover; - the amendment to article 18 of the directive, which concerns risk cover, which is a new amendment tabled by mrs herzog: it is a reasonable one. so our political assessment of this draft directive is that, once again, it is unbalanced against the objectives which are not only of a single market but also of social cohesion and solidarity.

- president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking mrs rothe for the excellent work she has done, which has laid the foundations for the first part: the commission has also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. what should be the focus of attention? firstly, on the 12% target to be reached by 2010. the temptation is, of course, to stress that it would have been stronger if it had been a binding percentage and not an indicative target as it is now. i am personally in favour of binding targets, provided that the targets are realistic and achievable. in view of the many unknown factors in the development of renewable energy, it seems to me therefore particularly sensible to set indicative targets on the one hand, but at the same time to allow the commission to make proposals with binding targets to the council and parliament if the results prove to be insufficient. the second point concerns the clear separation of waste from separate collection from waste from non-separated collection. of course, i can only consider the incineration of separate waste and more specifically the biodegradable fraction of industrial and domestic waste as a renewable energy source. opponents wrongly argue that burning household waste displaces the so-called true "renewables" such as wind and solar energy, increases co2 emissions and undermines waste reduction and recycling policies. it is therefore a matter of good judgment that the common position, in its definition of what should be considered as renewable energy and what should not, leaves room for different forms of renewable energy sources and, more specifically, for the incineration of separated waste. a position in which i can fully identify.

in writing. - (de) the joint draft directive of the european parliament and of the council on company law relating to takeover bids has been rejected by my group. the compromise reached in the conciliation committee was not acceptable in our view. the saying "all things come to those who wait" could have been applied to a perfect directive after 12 years of discussion. it's the opposite! where are the problems ? 1. the there are protected companies in the member states, whether by voting rights, preferential shares, multiple voting rights, etc. the directive states that protected companies may acquire protected companies, but that unprotected companies may not acquire protected companies. this means that there is no 'level playing field'. 2. the the principles for determining a measured price to be submitted in the event of a compulsory tender are missing, as are the principles concerning the rights of majority shareholders wishing to acquire the minority shareholders' shares. 3. the european companies would be subject to pressure from the major european financial groups, simply because the emphasis would no longer be on value added but exclusively on profit maximisation. this further increases the risk that acquisitions carried out in such a model will lead to massive job losses. 4. the the rights of the trade unions to co-manage are not sufficiently articulated. the duty to inform does not guarantee that trade union organisations can influence the procurement process. the commission is also aware of these problems. the commission therefore proposes to ask a group of experts in company law to discuss in-depth harmonisation of company law and to clarify its position on article 9 of the directive on takeover bids before march 2002. what does that logic mean? i think it would have been more sensible to have first convened a group of experts, presented the results of their work, and then only promulgated the directive, instead of promulgating the directive and then allowing a group of experts to debate the unresolved problems of this directive. in addition to the directive on public tenders, there is an urgent need to adopt a directive on the duty of neutrality. this has been in draft form since 1972 and has not yet been put to the vote. we expressly ask mr bolkestein and the council to do their homework. it is because we wanted to give them that opportunity that we rejected the document presented.
in writing. - (nl) to my great regret, i had to vote against the conciliation committee's outcome on the company law directive on takeover bids. i regret that my vote of no to the proposal is part of sacrificing so many years of work. however, our evaluation criterion must be the quality of the final product. and the end product is not satisfactory, among other things because it considerably weakens the position of european companies vis-à-vis us public tenders and, in particular, makes them weaker than those of us entrepreneurs whose company would be the target of a european buyer. furthermore, the directive as a whole focuses on only one aspect, namely capital increase, while other protection techniques or instruments are not harmonised. those who think that this proposal will treat european companies on an equal footing are therefore mistaken. i regret that these reasons, in all honesty, left me no choice but to vote against the proposal.

commission. – the honourable member refers to an inquiry launched by the commission on 13 june following press articles on europol. in order to avoid a possible misunderstanding the commission will have to point out that it has not launched an inquiry into the europol-related matter to which the honourable member refers. as far as the commission is aware the matter concerns accusations of fraud unrelated to the sensus contract. the commission understands that internal investigations by europol have led to the dutch judicial authorities now investigating the matter. as far as the action the commission has undertaken concerning the sensus project is concerned, the commission has initiated an internal review of the management of this project as indicated in the commission's reply to the oral questions by the honourable member. following this review and also on the basis of ongoing, more general recommendations, both from the court of auditors and the external monitoring panel of the information society technologies programme, the commission is implementing changes in procedures which will ensure significant improvements in project management. the commission attends the meetings of the management board of europol and meetings of the heads of national units in an observer capacity. it has no mandate to investigate europol other than in the context of direct contractor relations such as through the sensus project. in the context of sensus and as explained in the replies to written questions, the commission is not currently planning a financial audit of europol's participation in sensus. but the need for such an audit will be assessed again when all the cost claims related to sensus have been submitted by project participants. that should happen in the next few months. sensus and aventinus were involved in pre-competitive research using many basic technologies for the production of a demonstrator. the objective was to overcome linguistic barriers between european law enforcement agencies in their fight against organised crime such as drugs smuggling and illegal immigration. sensus is an assembly of software models including linguistic content, utilisation and report generation, workflow and database management systems. there is therefore no specific basic technology. according to information received by the commission, although polygon was originally intended to be used in sensus, in the autumn of 1999 a different technology was selected instead. the commission service has been assured by the contractors that polygon is not a component of the sensus demonstrator. the coordinator of the aventinus projects has confirmed that polygon was not used in either aventinus i or aventinus ii. as far as technologies used in europolis and eurint systems are concerned, the commission is not in a position to address the question of the honourable member.

- madam president-in-office of the council, commissioner, i believe that the g8 summit in genoa was an important moment in our political life and probably marked a turning point in european and world political life, unfortunately not because of the commitments made by the richest countries of the world to respond to the serious problems facing the poorest countries - i would point out that the imbalances between north and south have never been greater - but rather because of the context of this summit itself. mr vitorino rightly referred to a number of decisions or at least guidelines adopted at that summit, but i do not think that they should be given any importance that they do not have. it is true that there has been a timid recognition of the relevance of debt relief for the poorest countries, but we have not gone further. it is also true that a global fund for the control of infectious diseases has been set up, but with a total funding that is far from sufficient, since the budget has been set at 1.3 billion euros, whereas the un secretary-general considers that 10 billion euros would be needed. however, there is no sign of a common will to redress the inequalities, including by achieving the long-promised target of devoting 0.7% of gross domestic product to development aid. we're not talking about it anymore. the participants, however, strongly reaffirmed their belief in the virtues of the market, free trade, the development of new technologies in genetic engineering and the rules of international trade. the european union has certainly been keen to launch a new round of the wto to date, but is this the answer to the problems posed by the unfairness of the wto rules? the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking all the members who took part in the debate on my report in committee, and in particular the fictitious rapporteurs from the various political groups, mrs oomen-ruijten, mr bowe, mrs schörling and mr sjöstedt. the commission has therefore tabled a twenty-fourth proposal to amend the 1976 dangerous substances directive, which proposes a ban on the placing on the market and use of pentabromodiphenyl ether. what exactly are we talking about? pentabromodiphenyl ether is a flame retardant which is used to 95% in the manufacture of flexible polyurethane foam for furniture and upholstery. it is found, for example, in car headrests and in the protective and packaging foams of electrical and electronic components. as far as penta is concerned, first of all, and its harmful effects on our environment and our health, the facts are clear, very clear. a first study carried out in 1998 by the swedish authorities showed a high concentration of the substance in breast milk. two opinions of the scientific committee on toxicity and ecotoxicity and the environment, dated february and june 2000, highlight the potential harm caused by penta, the release of this substance into the environment as a persistent and bioaccumulative substance. so risk to the environment, risk to human health. all the opinions are in agreement. it is therefore quite logical that the commission proposed in january this year to ban the placing on the market and use of penta. and it is just as logical, i would say, that our committee on the environment has voted to confirm this ban. the second substance, octar-bromodiphenyl ether, is directly concerned by the directive because of the percentage of penta-residue it contains when placed on the market. octa is mostly used as a flame retardant here in plastic supplies and office equipment. the commission, in this case, preferred to rely on the final conclusions of the risk assessment study which, for this substance, is still ongoing. it therefore limited the percentage of penta residue allowed in the octa to 5%. the committee on the environment, i should like to say, does not share this view. it seems to us to be a complete contradiction to ban penta in the first place and to allow it to be present in significant quantities, as we see it in the composition of other chemicals, other substances. i would also say that the initial conclusions of the risk assessment study which is currently under way, and which i have been able to read, are negative and would prove the harmfulness of this product to both the environment and public health, which are being dealt with by the british and french experts respectively, whom i have consulted at length on this subject. in view of these various factors, mr president, the committee on the environment has tabled an amendment to extend the ban on the marketing of octar-bromodiphenyl ether to include it, and this in strict compliance with the precautionary principle. i should like to remind you that caution is not to wait for the absolute evidence, that is to say the final result of the evaluation study, when there is sufficient evidence that a substance could have harmful consequences for health and the environment. that's exactly what the octa is. thirdly, and this is not a two-way street, decabromodiphenyl ether, which is a flame retardant of the same family, actually represents 80% of this bromine family and has many applications, not only in electrical and electronic equipment but also in insulation, construction and textiles. deca is the only one of these three substances not mentioned in the commission's text. it seems to me, however, difficult, indeed impossible, and i shall return to this in a moment, to disregard deca, even though i ask in amendment no 4 of my report for a method of assessing the risks by family rather than by individual substance. for reasons of consistency, the committee on the environment has extended the scope of the text to all polybrominated diphenyl ethers. in this respect it fully respects the vote of our assembly on the directive on electrical and electronic equipment. pending specific information on the risk assessment study on deca, which is still ongoing, amendment no 16, which i tabled with the epp and socialist groups, proposes in substance a ban on deca as from 1 january 2006, unless the risk assessment were to somehow whitewash the substance and therefore conclude that deca would not be a problem. this is a kind of reversibility clause. and since i am referring to the amendments, i should like to say that i am against amendment no 9, which seeks to amend the title of the proposal in order to extend the immediate ban on the marketing of deca. i do not really have any philosophical objections to amendments nos 12, 13 and 14, but i prefer the commission's original text. as regards the other amendments tabled by the green group and the united left, i believe that my report already takes into account the criticisms made in amendment no 11 concerning the extreme burden and slowness of the procedures for risk assessment and control of existing substances. i was just saying a word about it. in conclusion, the council's working party on dangerous substances, which meets in one week's time, will set out the broad guidelines to be adopted by the internal market council on 27 september. i hope that they will help to bring parliament's and the commission's positions closer together. i also hope, and i think it is quite possible, to finalise the adoption of this twenty-fourth proposal for amending the dangerous substances directive at second reading.
- the president, ladies and gentlemen, i would also like to join in the congratulations to the rapporteur. everyone has said that this is a very difficult dossier because it involves various interests with large lobbies, but also fire safety, and the protection and future of the environment in the long term. it is probably a very technical debate. the following table lists the active substances and their derivatives: what's in a name? it is a matter of fire retardants containing bromine, but it is also a matter of great social relevance. the previous speakers have clearly demonstrated the problems this poses for the environment and for public health. octabde and decabde were first on the first list of priority substances for risk analysis under a risk analysis regulation seven years ago. the evaluations are still not completed. since these substances are known to be dangerous to the environment, waiting for the conclusions of this slow risk analysis would unnecessarily delay the improvement of the protection of man and the environment. even though i would have preferred an immediate ban on brominated flame retardants - and my colleagues know that - i am fully in favour of the compromise because it is also acceptable. it is a question of an immediate ban on penta- and octa-bde and a ban in 2006, unless - and this is not insignificant - proof of harmlessness is provided. it is also clear that this dossier puts us on the cusp between the old policy based on risk analysis and the new chemical policy we are now discussing, which should allow for faster analyses, but also that the responsibility for this is no longer with the commission and the member states but with the producers. this dossier shows once again that the current chemical policy with a dust-by-dust analysis takes years and that the government is in a very weak position to protect people and the environment. we therefore urge an urgent review of it, as we are doing now with the white paper on chemical policy.
- mr president, i would first of all like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr nisticò, on the thoroughness and quality of his work, and on his openness and cooperation with the fictitious rapporteurs. this directive is essential. it sets out, for the first time in community legislation, the quality and safety requirements for blood products, whether intended for transfusion or for processing into medicinal products. it also allows the free movement of donors and blood products within the union. i would therefore say that the text is ambitious and overall satisfactory. i would add that the main improvements brought about by the commission's vote all go in the same direction: the strengthening of control and inspection measures throughout the whole transfusion chain, in order to protect donors from human error and recipients from the risks of exposure to potentially contaminated blood donations. however, another threat to patients today is the shortage of plasma and plasma derivatives in the eu. that is why the liberal group is opposed to the absolute and immediate obligation to donate free of charge, that is to say to amendments nos 55 and 56, which, by the way, also seem to go against any notion of subsidiarity. first, because it is not the unpaid gift that is in danger. at no time have we questioned the principle of voluntary work, or the irreplaceable social role played by the voluntary donor. it is not the unpaid gift that is in question, but this free obligation. and above all, because these amendments royally ignore the concerns of the patients. we have consulted at length with patients who are particularly concerned, and i use a euphemism, about the increasing dependence on american imports, particularly plasma, and with haemophiliacs who remember that free treatment has not protected them from viral contamination. we shouldn't have the best as the enemy of the good. clearly, obviously, it is imperative that blood not be a source of profit. we have said this, and we have repeated it, in several recitals in the compromise amendment which we tabled with the epp. this is an objective which the member states must achieve under the conditions and timing of amendment no 75, but to make this free of charge an absolute condition for giving blood would be a disaster for patients, and i am speaking of patients and not of industrialists, mr lund, because it is the haemophiliacs in particular who we have consulted and heard at length about. what really matters here is that the blood is of the highest quality and the directive ensures that, but what would quality and safety be without the availability of blood, i ask you. i would remind you that there are 7 million blood donors in europe out of 360 million citizens. i conclude, mr president, and that is why i call on all the political groups to support this compromise amendment which ensures free access for patients to blood and their medicines, blood and medicines which save thousands of lives every day.
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. in this connection, i should like to congratulate mr camisón asensio on his report, in which he stresses that petitions allow citizens to make known both their legitimate aspirations and the shortcomings inherent in community legislation, and from this dual point of view the commission is certainly interested in the work that you are doing. and as mrs gonzález said earlier, i believe that the committee on petitions in this parliament - let me say this from the outside - as well as in other parliaments where i have been (the parliament of my country, for example, which is the one i know best) plays a very important role in bringing citizens closer to the institutions in general. but they are also important for the work of the members themselves, to know what concerns, sensitivities and issues are of concern to a significant part of the citizens. this year it was not only the commission services who cooperated with this committee, but we, the commissioners themselves, also took care to cooperate actively with you. i would like to thank mr gemelli for his reference, because it is true that mr kinnock, mr liikanen, mrs wallström and mr bolkestein have all taken part in the work of the committee, have personally discussed, explained and tried to provide reasons, possibilities or solutions to the concerns raised by the committee on petitions in the light of the complaints or grievances raised by various citizens. i think it is clear from this that the commission attaches great importance to the work that you are doing. in this same spirit - and i have had occasion to comment on this subject before - the commission will respond in the most open and constructive way possible to its desire to strengthen this cooperation. i know that the time limits in which we sometimes reply to some of the information which is requested from us is a problem, but the honourable members know very well that when we have had a problem, normally - if not 100% of the time - the problem was that the commission depended on the information which a member state had to supply. they're different administrations. we have no coercive capacity to force member states to send us certain types of information, except political pressure. we do this and we exercise it - as do the honourable members - when we complain that a state has not replied or is making it difficult for the commission to reply. all i can say is that the commission will continue to try to get the states of the union to act as quickly as possible in their response to the information we request, precisely in order to be able to respond adequately to the concerns which your honourable members have expressed to us, and that in this regard we will continue to try to cooperate as much as possible with your honourable members.
the rapporteur, mr president, is calling for a discussion of the breakfast directives at the end of the morning. it's a little late, but i'm gonna take my chances. i would remind you that this is the second time we have been consulted on this matter. in the previous parliamentary term, parliament had spoken on the commission's proposals for the simplification of vertical directives on sugar, honey, fruit juices, milk and jams. it is true that the council took a long time to finalise the dossier and finally made significant changes to the commission's initial proposals. that is why we are being consulted again today. i would therefore remind you that the aim was to simplify the guidelines. the view of the committee on the environment and consumer protection is certainly compatible with this objective, but it also means ensuring that quality requirements are not relaxed in the name of simplification, and above all that good consumer information is not compromised. all the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, many of which were tabled by the rapporteur, are in this direction. i would like to remind you that the consensus in the council obviously limits our room for manoeuvre, since we are merely being consulted, that is to say that beyond the vote we are going to take today, the council will, in the end, have completely free hands to decide. nevertheless, i believe that the amendments succeed in improving the texts in the light of what i said earlier: quality of products, correct information for the consumer. i shall now review the various directives and give the main points. first of all, on sugars, there is a little problem of definition. the council has rightly defined a sugar which is increasingly being used, fructose, but it has failed to give a definition of what it calls brown sugar or red sugar. the amendment by parliament is therefore aimed at asking for a definition in the texts. we must impose a certain time limit, but the commission must make a proposal for a definition. that sounds basic. second case, the honey. this is a sensitive issue, since honey is a high-quality product, generally handmade, and there have been many marketing problems in previous years, since there are often mixtures of honey and honey arriving on the eu market whose quality is not guaranteed. it is therefore a matter of being vigilant about definitions and labelling. the commission proposes to add a new category of honey, filtered honey. the committee on the environment proposes not to accept this proposal, since filtered honey is in fact honey whose floral origin can no longer be controlled. most of the time, it has lost the pollen that allows this control, which prevents a good definition of the product and goes against a guarantee of quality for the consumer. we therefore propose not to accept this proposal. as regards the dangerous substances which might be found in industrial or pastry honey, we think that they should be banned. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of honey is not compromised. so there is an amendment from the committee on the environment to that effect. the third case concerns fruit juices. i believe that most of the amendments are not intended to change the commission and council proposal in any substantial way, but rather to clarify the texts and avoid any drift. i can explain why: a non-exhaustive list of processes and substances permitted in manufacture, as envisaged, does not seem satisfactory to us. we need to give a positive list. we therefore ask the commission to submit, within a certain time, a positive list of permitted filtration and precipitation adjuvants and absorption adjuvants. a positive list means that substances not on the list are prohibited. we think that this will be clearer in the long term and more positive for the consumer. the commission is also proposing that the use of fungicides should be restricted to the preservation of fruit intended for the production of juices, and that only cold preservation be allowed. as regards the fourth dossier, milk, there are no amendments. the text proposed to us seems to us to be quite satisfactory. i have personally reintroduced an amendment on jams which was not accepted by the committee on the environment, but which i support. the aim is to reject the widespread use of a chemical flavouring in jams and marmalades, namely vanillin. it's not really good for anything, except to change the taste.
- mr president, it is almost five years since the council consulted us on the proposal to simplify seven vertical directives on foodstuffs. the aim of these proposals was to simplify or replace the existing arrangements, which contained overly detailed provisions, with directives which merely incorporated the essential requirements. in this way they would be more workable and more in line with the horizontal directives. that is a noble aspiration, and one which is in line with the expectations of the people, both consumers and those otherwise involved. we in the epp group are very happy to cooperate on this. we note that we were the first to complete the directive on cocoa and chocolate products, which was originally included in this package and which had to undergo the longest legislative procedure, namely the co-decision procedure. the five other directives in the package of seven, the five we are discussing today, we are finally doing more than five years of simplified work. we're still wondering if we should be looking for faster ways of working, and other ways of making decisions, because we don't think that's what people are asking us to do. president. -
