- mr president, the practice of genital mutilation is an extreme form of oppression of women, which goes back far into the history of some countries. it is the women of those countries themselves, of egypt, of somalia, who have made us aware of their extreme suffering. they sued, and they had to overcome a world of prejudice to do so, but they did it for the sake of their sisters, for the sake of their little sisters often, for the sake of their own daughters. they ask us to have the same courage. a number of women have sent a petition to the european parliament in the same vein. we know we're supported by women all over the world. respect for the human person must be assessed in the same way for men and women: women's rights are human rights. it is not by allowing this mutilation to continue under medically acceptable conditions that the mutilation itself becomes acceptable. it is not because this inhuman practice is one of the oldest traditions in some communities - i say deliberately not religions, because it has little to do with them - that we should tolerate it. the sexual mutilation of girls must be considered a criminal offence in all countries, in all societies, as called for in various international conventions. on the positive side, however, more and more countries have banned female genital mutilation. but in many cases this prohibition is a dead letter and we should therefore amend the cotonou agreement so that we can apply it in the same way as we do in the case of human rights violations. i agree with the other members' views.
the rapporteur. - mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, despite all the legal instruments at european level and in the member states prohibiting wage discrimination, there are still significant and persistent pay gaps between men and women. the figures provided by very interesting european studies indicate a pay gap of 25 to 28%, and in some countries even 30%. the pay gap is also greater in the private sector than in the public sector, greater in the industrial sector than in agriculture or the service sector and greatest in those sectors where women are underrepresented. it is not always easy to analyse the meaning of these figures correctly. but it is widely accepted that about half of the wage gap is due to the different career paths of men and women - for example, women take a much easier break in their careers than men, with a consequent delay in their careers - to the lower qualifications of mainly older working women, to the fact that working women are on average younger than working men, in other words to objective structural differences. but the other half of the pay gap is not objectively explainable and points to the presence of hidden discriminatory mechanisms that lead to the undervaluation of female-dominated positions and occupations. the main mechanism is the job ranking mechanism, whereby the different jobs are ranked by weight, placed in a particular class and are assigned a salary according to that class. the evaluation of the function is usually carried out by the social partners, whether or not on the basis of a system developed by a consultancy firm. discrimination in job evaluation is expressed in undervaluing characteristics traditionally associated with female roles, such as social skills, greater concentration and greater agility of women. the fact that they receive fewer points, are less appreciated, places these positions too low in the hierarchy and salary scales and thus lowers the wages for women. on the other hand, there is an overestimation of characteristics associated with traditional male functions, such as technical insight, physical hard work or financial responsibility. in general, employees do not know how their position was classified. it is even questionable whether the social partners within the companies have any insight into the way in which the functions are classified. in addition, women are generally not involved in job evaluation, since they are not the negotiators with the social partners. then the question is: what can europe do about this? i think, madam commissioner, that first of all the statistical data collection for the wages of men and women and also for the explanation of the wage differentials must be improved. at present, the collection of statistical data is inadequate both at european level and in most member states. so that's the first thing that should happen. i know that the commission has appointed a group of experts to start with, but what i am asking is that they also look for an explanation for the wage differentials. secondly, the european union could take a closer look at the problem of discrimination in job evaluation. to this end, an initiative could be taken to supplement, for example, the 1975 directive on equal pay with an annex setting out a number of rules and criteria to ensure a gender-neutral system of job evaluation. the member states need a guide. the social partners also need it. i think europe can be a perfect help in this. in addition, the social partners must be engaged to ensure transparency in the value scales used in the wage formation process. how can people know whether their pay is discriminated against if there is no transparency in the way it is formed? the social partners should also commit themselves to involving more women in wage negotiations, especially to eliminate discrimination, and finally to put wage discrimination at the top of their agenda, because that never happens. the employment guidelines must also be tightened up, with quantitative objectives, target dates and targets. they are not in it, nor in the new guidelines, even though they are already much better than the previous ones. it needs to be monitored much more closely. i have never heard the commission complain that the national action plans do not pay enough attention to this problem. in short, there is a need for a global policy strategy. all these elements are part of this, including the campaign which the commission intends to carry out and which could be a useful element in bringing some of these issues to the fore. so my question is: i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the situation in the community.
the commission has also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. - mr president, first of all i should like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr andersson, on his report. it has tackled a new subject which is more or less regulated in only a few member states. it is good that europe is leading the way in a number of areas, as is very often the case. so congratulations to you. why did the committee on women's rights give its opinion on this specifically? of course, it is a problem affecting both men and women, but our committee has found that women are more often, much more often, victims of this kind of harassment and, in addition, victims of another kind of harassment, which very often even leads to sexual harassment, and that therefore it had its own specific role to play. that is why our committee has given its own opinion. i think we should all be aware that this is a new problem. we can learn from the measures taken by many member states in the field of sexual violence, because devising a policy to deal with sexual harassment at work is not so easy and not so obvious. but there are a number of member states that are trying to do it and we have experience in many member states of taking action against sexual violence. what can we do? firstly, there is the preventive approach, which means that companies must make it clear to their employees that they will not accept harassment at work. that's a lot of preemptive action. secondly, a confidential person can be appointed to mediate between the two parties if a problem arises. it is still preferable in a company to reach a solution through mediation rather than one of the parties having to go to court. i prefer this type of solution. thirdly, if it is difficult to find a solution, management can impose sanctions. fourthly, the possibility of bringing legal proceedings must be preserved in any event. this seems to me to be a series of practical matters for us to take care of.
- the proposal for a regulation (com (89) 6 final) on the common organization of the market in bananas; what should have been an annual debate on the implementation of schengen cooperation is now becoming more topical as the council of justice and home affairs ministers debates european measures to combat international terrorism. no one will be able to deny that schengen could play a role in this. i am reluctant to make calls, as i did in the meeting yesterday, because of the right-wing calls for the free movement of persons to be buried once and for all and their call for the installation of big brother conditions. the debate on schengen in my group has always been about a necessary but difficult balance between the right to free movement of persons, respect for the rule of law and human rights and the right to security through police and judicial cooperation, and i want that to remain the case. but despite the fact that schengen has been in existence for six years now as a forerunner of the area of freedom, justice and law, we must note in mr coelho's report that there are still many things that are lacking. i would like to mention three. firstly, schengen exists, and yet we must note that there is still no effective police cooperation, no effective exchange of information that can produce serious results in the fight against organised crime. the annual report does indeed state that there is clear improvement, but the question is whether the priorities are being put in the right place. to put it bluntly: is the schengen information system really being used in the fight against international crime and terrorism as defined in the un convention? does it not serve primarily as an information system against unwanted migration? let me ask that question again today, with all that is coming out. secondly, schengen has been used in the context of events connected with european and international summits. no democrat will object to the fact that rioters are stopped from coming to wreak havoc. but the way in which peaceful protesters have been stopped by internal border controls and information exchange based on the sis is really too far for anyone who stands for freedom of expression. thirdly, police cooperation and information exchange require proper scrutiny by the judiciary and parliaments. schengen is going to go off the rails. it goes so far that the council has decided not to publish any more reports, since schengen is now so to speak part of the european acquis, as if schengen ceased to exist. my group would certainly like to see schengen replaced by transparent, democratically controlled european rules, but in the meantime we want the european parliament to monitor schengen together with the national parliaments.
- mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of the liberal group i would like to join the colleagues who have preceded me in expressing our solidarity with the families, the relatives of the victims and the people of toulouse as a whole. in the present state of the available judicial information and pending the completion of the investigation which is under way, which will determine the causes of the disaster, what lessons, mr president, can we already draw from this deadly explosion? first, and it hurts, very much, history repeats itself. thirty-three years after the seveso disaster, to mention just one, two european directives aimed at providing the member states with a harmonized policy for the management of major industrial risks will not have been sufficient to avoid a new human tragedy, a cruel reality which shows the limits of a law, even if it is binding and transnational. we should avoid responding to this industrial catastrophe with a seveso iii directive. the liberals are, first and foremost, and the commissioner has said this, in favour of all member states strictly applying the provisions of existing legislation, in particular the seveso ii directive, which dates from december 1996. and we are far from that, with infringement proceedings being brought against six member states, including france. you have just also mentioned, mrs wallström, that no member state had transposed the directive in time. for example, has the industrial owner of the site, as explicitly required by an article in seveso ii, updated the hazard study in the last five years? this is a question that arises from the fact that at a seminar held in france in 1999, the european union's network for respect for environmental rights had questioned the origins of a major ammonia leak at the grande paroisse plant, classified as seveso ii, and had pointed out the defects in design and equipment reliability. it is clear that the precautionary principle has not prevailed here. it should be noted that this hazard study is the essential basis for any preventive and disaster management strategy of any kind. i come now to the central point of this debate: the control of urbanisation - mrs berès has just spoken about this - around sites. the entire population, employees of the factory, of course, but also the residents of the surrounding area, were the victims of the massive chemical explosion. the azf plant was nothing more or less than a time bomb located less than five kilometres from the centre of toulouse, which has a population of 400,000. this example of a fast-moving conurbation is far from unique in europe. if we draw two lines, one from london to milan and the other from copenhagen to barcelona, and 200 km of corridors on either side of the branches of this x, we find that this demarcated territory contains 65% of our population and 80% of the industrial sites in the union, including dangerous sites. in france, the national debate is on the question of urban risk. beyond this unprecedented tragedy that toulouse has just experienced, 360 million european citizens are waiting for a real spatial planning policy, here and now.
- mr president, commissioner, there is no doubt that after the dramatic events of 11 september, asylum and immigration policies will undergo drastic changes. in view of this, it might have been more appropriate to postpone the examination of these reports, since they do not take account of recent developments at international level. particular attention should be paid to the wording of paragraph 15 of the pirker report. the present text emphasizes that member states are obliged to respect the rights and obligations of third-country nationals. this is a bridge too far, in my opinion. integration cannot be a two-way street unless there is reciprocity and mutual recognition. in other words, only if reciprocity and mutual recognition can be enforced in the third countries can it be integrated in both directions.
- mr president, i thank you for giving me the floor after all this debate, which was very rich. i believe that these two reports are now proof that we need a common policy on migration and asylum. i would like to welcome the commissioner's statement and all the work of parliament, which really shows the practical interest we take in these matters, especially in the present circumstances. i think that here today we have been able to distinguish one from the other and perhaps also distinguish asylum from economic migration. i'm not sure all the amalgamations were avoided. on migration flows and the right to asylum, we must, against all odds, affirm that rights and duties are to be shared, both on the part of migrants and on the part of the host member states. it is not that some people are taking advantage of the shortcomings of our harmonization to create sub-category europeans by creating second-class workers or suspended citizens. i think it is important that the rights of workers, whatever their origin, be affirmed and made concrete in order to combat human trafficking, the exploitation of poverty and distress by some. asylum policy will gain in quality and justice if it is agreed that it must be supplemented by other mandatory policies of cooperation which enable everyone to live in dignity and respect in his own country. in any case, asylum policy must reject arbitrary criteria linked to current events. i would simply ask that political rights be granted to those whom we welcome for their work.
- (fr) mr president, first of all i should like to congratulate mr titley on his very good and constructive report. his efforts, together with the constructive contributions of other colleagues, have ensured that a consensus has been reached in this parliament on a subject which is very sensitive: the question of the right to vote. the control of the arms trade. in the wake of the attacks in america, there is a growing awareness that the massive proliferation of weapons around the world is creating unstable countries that are breeding grounds for terrorism. the titley report rightly points out that the 1998 eu code is an important step towards a more coherent and transparent european arms export policy. but at the same time, it points out a number of shortcomings and gaps, of which i would like to mention five. firstly, the annual reports of the member states are not harmonised. some countries, such as the united kingdom and ireland, give a detailed description of the licences issued and of the quantity and value of the arms exported. other countries, such as austria and greece, do not provide any information on the licences issued. in this way, efficient, effective evaluation is of course difficult. the annual report should therefore meet a number of standards. each licence issued should be accompanied by a detailed description of the equipment, the intended use and the end user. however, for authorisations that are refused, the reason for refusal should be explained in more detail. secondly, i also believe that there should be better control over the end user of the exported weapons. in africa, the official end users of arms from bulgaria are togo and burkina, while in reality they are being funneled to liberia and the rebels in sierra leone. eu member states' embassies could play a stronger control role on the ground. there should also be penalties for transit countries that violate arms embargoes. thirdly, i believe that there should be better control over the licensed production of military equipment abroad by eu companies or in the framework of industrial cooperation agreements. it is not possible for a country to be denied a weapons licence and for arms manufacturers to circumvent that ban by supplying parts for weapons which are then assembled on site. a fourth shortcoming is that there is no control at all on private arms dealers, which have been increasing since the end of the cold war. of the 500 million small arms in circulation worldwide, 40 to 60 percent are illegal, according to the un. a final serious shortcoming of the eu code of conduct is that it is not legally binding. right now, it's a non-binding gentlemen's agreement. there can be no sanction against those who violate the code. finally, mr president, i would like to express the hope that the eu code of conduct will form the basis for a global code on arms exports. it is quite pointless for europe to be working on controlling the arms trade while china and russia continue to export arms without restriction to crisis areas. the un conference in july was a missed opportunity in this respect and i believe that this subject must remain a permanent part of the transatlantic dialogue.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is of course good that there is such a report on the arms trade. it is also good that there is a european code of conduct. but i hope that the dramatic circumstances we are experiencing today will also give us the opportunity to go one step further. for example, to make the code of conduct enforceable, as mr titley rightly points out in his report. and finally to make progress on arms control. because we say, control the arms trade, but if you don't control the end user certificate, then you can't control whether that arms trade is legal or not. licensed production by eu countries in third countries is also a theme. we must finally get negative lists in which we do not supply weapons under any circumstances, and that is checked and sanctioned.

- mr president, rarely has a un conference been as well-intentioned and as poorly understood as durban. i think that has to do in part with the lack of preparation. diplomats and officials should have gone further, i believe, in reaching a consensus beforehand. that would have saved the world community a lot of money, a lot of time and a lot of embarrassing scenes. it is particularly unfortunate that a number of delegations have deserted, for whatever their good reasons, deserters are always wrong. in this context it was all the more surprising that, thanks to the stubbornness of louis michel - as we know him in belgium, as the world now knows him - a good final statement has still come out of the bus. a final statement containing a number of important provisions which, if applied, could profoundly change the lives of hundreds of thousands, millions of people. governments are urged to allow minorities to practice their own language, culture and religion, to act against police forces that behave racist and to adjust their legal systems so that they do not discriminate against certain groups. roma and gypsy children should receive equal education. in the field of migration policy, there must be no discrimination on the basis of race. the provisions on the middle east are also good and balanced, emphasizing both the right of the palestinians to an independent state and the security of israel. and finally, two centuries after the fact, europe has apologized for the slave trade. let us not forget that at least ten million africans have been taken to america in what is the largest forced migration in human history. millions more died in the slave trade or on one of the infamous cargo ships. the statements made by commissioner bolkestein in a dutch newspaper last weekend are therefore totally misplaced. "i am not satisfied with the apology of the un conference in durban", he said: "i never owned a slave myself, so why should i apologize?" such statements, mr president, are a sign of western arrogance, which ignores the fact that american prosperity is built partly on the labour of slaves and european prosperity is built partly on the raw materials of the former colonies. can this be an alibi for governments in developing countries to evade their responsibility and shift all blame to the past? no, not at all. but before we can lecture others on civilization, we must have the intellectual honesty to describe this chapter of our history as it was. a crime against humanity.

- commissioner, the european union is faced with several important challenges which it is necessary to address as quickly as possible if it is to carry out effective, coherent and long-term political action against terrorism, and i would say more particularly in the dismantling of terrorist networks. the first challenge has already been mentioned by several colleagues, and that is to avoid a clash of civilizations with the muslim world. and in this connection i take the opportunity of minister michel's presence to congratulate him on his diplomatic action in recent weeks, which i believe can only yield positive results in the constitution of a very broad coalition against terrorism and those who support it. the very broad coalition is, of course, about involving as many states as possible, but it is not about losing sight of the fact that the enemies of our enemies are not always our friends and that no state in this coalition is exempt from its duties in terms of democracy, the protection of human rights and respect for minorities. i am thinking in particular of russia and china, which are of course important players, but which also cause us major problems in this area. the second challenge, and it is not a small one, is to ensure better police and judicial cooperation without compromising the individual rights and freedoms of everyone. in the case of the european union, we are faced with a total lack of parliamentary control in this area. so i think, and i think we are quite unanimous on this, that it would be important for judicial and police cooperation to be integrated into the first pillar. that would certainly be an answer. the european union has also been involved in the negotiations for the establishment of the international criminal court, which has been in progress for several years now, and which is slowly coming to a halt. i would like to stress that we absolutely need such a court to try crimes against humanity such as those committed in new york and washington. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to promote the use of the euro, including the creation of a european monetary system, and the creation of a european monetary fund. the third challenge is the problem of dispossessing terrorist networks of their means of action, both financial and technical. we have already touched upon the subject of financial means, there is at the moment a draft directive on money laundering which is a little out of date, and i believe that there is an effort to be made in the dialogue between the council and parliament to bring about the adoption of this directive quickly, knowing that it is a first step, which is not sufficient but which is in any case decisive. in the long term, i am convinced that we will not escape a major action to lift banking secrecy and to abolish tax havens. money laundering is clearly a major weapon in the hands of international terrorist associations and international terrorist networks. i am struck by the fact that there is information, which has not been refuted, that even before the attacks in new york and washington it appeared that the shares of certain companies, i am thinking in particular of the two american air transport companies and several insurance companies which were affected by the attack, were sold en masse on the stock market before the accidents. we could speak of insider trading, but it seems to me that at least an international investigation would be necessary to verify this information. finally, on the technical side, when i say technical, which is a weak word when talking about arms, it is obvious that the european union must intensify its fight against the illegal and uncontrolled export of small arms and be attentive to a fundamental problem, which is the proliferation of chemical and bacteriological weapons. finally, i believe that we will not escape a longer-term reflection on the vulnerability of a number of our technological tools to terrorist acts. i am thinking, for example, of the energy risks posed by nuclear power stations, most of which are very vulnerable to action of the kind that has been taken in the united states.
- after 9/11, the world is in a state of tension. people are shocked. they sympathize with the victims and their families and feel less safe than ever before. people expect the government to guarantee their safety. they realize that this cannot be done in an absolute way, but they rightly call for adequate and efficient action. whoever is responsible for security today has a very complex task. the need for genuine european integration and international coordination is clearer than ever, both in deeds and words. i hope very much, mr president, that you will be able to implement your action plan of 21 september effectively and that the belgian presidency will be able to put forward concrete decisions on this matter. mr president, we have all said that the attacks were not so much aimed at the innocent victims as at symbols of freedom, democracy and human rights, such as the right to life. no one will deny that the european parliament is also a symbol of this. we do not want to be a target, nor do we want to cause panic, but many members - as mr poettering has already pointed out - are concerned. in strasbourg, the entrances to and around this building are guarded by the police. in brussels, on the other hand, we are denied even the illusion of security. moreover, if we hear in the interim that the belgian minister of the interior is coming here to say that he has no indication of the need for this, then we have real reason for concern. terrorists do not give advance directives, and a home secretary should be aware of that. i would therefore ask you, mr president-in-office of the council and foreign minister, to change your mind about your minister of the interior and the police in belgium. not only in the interests of the safety of the representatives of the people of 15 member states, but also of their staff, their visitors, the safety of the city, which is the capital of europe, and of the values which we all defend together.
mr president, commissioner, madam president-in-office of the council, i would like to echo the words of mr van velzen on the subject of mr bolkestein. as regards the debate on terrorism, i agree with the words of mr de berg, mr de rossa and others who have pointed out the broader approach. but since i was absent from the previous debate, i shall return to that debate. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on terrorism, and i would like to say that we must also prevent the growth or persistence of frustration in europe. that has to do with the enlargement. it is absolutely essential that the political criteria of democracy, human rights and minority rights are respected as much as the quantitative aspects of enlargement. the enlargement will include several small countries, the czech republic, slovakia and the baltic states. we therefore ask ourselves what the future position and rights of the constitutional regions will be, some of which see themselves as nations on an equal footing with others who have their own state. they must have a place of their own. i am thinking not only of flanders but also of scotland, wales, the basque country, galicia and others. i am thinking of all those constitutional regions which might feel obliged to seek the status of a member state because, in europe, only member states are respected. in other words, if we do not give the regions and peoples a place in europe in a future debate, we are in fact encouraging separatism. i wish you wouldn't. i hope that the regions will be able to contribute to the construction of a federal europe, a federal europe which is often held back by the member states themselves. i therefore urge that a paragraph on the regions be included.
- mr president, madam president, commissioner, i think that a lot has already been said on the question of the stability pact. i think that the main difference between the two joint motion for a resolution before the house is that the motion for a resolution which we have co-signed with the socialist group places more emphasis on the possibility of pursuing a countercyclical policy, that is to say, a policy of support for growth, within the limits of the stability pact. if i have heard the presidency's statement correctly, it seems to me that it supports the idea that the stability pact should make it possible, at the level of the european union, to pursue a counter-cyclical policy in terms of supporting growth. that said, i think we should not get our hopes up. the margin of manoeuvre in terms of monetary and budgetary policy is relatively limited, and i would like to add to the debate a point which has not yet been mentioned but which seems to me, in any case, to be of concern. as you know, mr commissioner, madam president, virtually all the member states are engaged in a structural reduction of public revenue, expressed as a percentage of gdp, through tax relief, and this in a context of tax competition between member states, particularly through corporation tax, and in a context of low growth. this type of policy risks putting downward pressure on us to reduce public expenditure structurally, leading either to an insufficient level of expenditure, given the model of solidarity which we advocate, or to an inequitable distribution of the funding of public expenditure. hence the importance of the debate on the development of other tax bases - and i think the presidency has alluded to this and is paying attention to it. and in this sense, the tobin tax is also an element that can help to widen the taxable base, but madam president, commissioner, i would like to ask you about the state of play within the prima renault group and about the state of play of the discussions on corporate taxation, because it seems to me that there is an urgent need to get out of the impasse in which the 15 member states are now. our committee on economic and monetary affairs is no longer informed of the progress of this work and when we hear back from it, it is to note that nothing has been done. in the context of slowing growth, it seems to me that the european union must act urgently and coordinate the development of corporate taxation. the second point i wanted to raise is terrorism and money laundering. we have basically the financial action task force on money laundering, the fatf, and the question of the money laundering directive. experts believe that progress in international cooperation has so far been limited and ineffective. the fatf has produced a set of recommendations, the implementation of which is left to the discretion of the signatory states. as early as october 1996, the geneva appeal, signed by european magistrates, called for the ratification of a number of international conventions, as well as the lifting of banking secrecy during investigative tasks. one of these magistrates recently repeated this call by calling for an international conference with a threefold objective: the definition of rules for the transparency of economic life, including the lifting of banking secrecy and the abolition of tax havens, judicial cooperation between states and finally a mechanism of sanctions against states which do not comply with these provisions. i ask the president and the commission to ensure that these requests from the judges, which have been renewed - i read it again in the press this morning - by the head of the fatf, are taken up by the belgian presidency so that work can be accelerated and the fatf recommendations made binding. this would be a useful role for the european union, both as the chair and as a major hub of the fatf. i think that from this point of view we could also move from speeches to deeds. i also believe that, as regards the tax package, which also includes binding provisions for tax havens or privileged schemes that exist within the european union itself, the combination of the fight against financial crime and the savings tax programme at european level is a political opportunity to be seized to accelerate this work. unfortunately, the informal ecofin council has once again found that we are not making any progress in this area.
mr president, i would like to ask the commissioner whether he has not already made a statement on the matter, and whether he has not made a statement on the matter. for the language arrangements, the common approach is that the system should be based on a number of principles which apply to everyone, including the principle of non-discrimination. in order to reduce translation costs, further consideration should be given to the various suggestions made in this connection, including technological translation support, without excluding other options a priori. on the basis of these guidelines, the council has recently begun to examine the relevant provisions proposed in the commission's proposal for a regulation on the community patent and the other solutions envisaged by the member states. in any event, the honourable member will understand that the council does not wish to make a clearer statement at this stage on this proposal for a regulation, including its linguistic aspects, as long as the european parliament, which has been consulted in accordance with article 308 of the treaty, has not itself delivered an opinion. the council would remind the honourable member that the language arrangements of the office for harmonization in the internal market are laid down in the regulation on the community trade mark, a regulation adopted unanimously by the council following the agreement reached by the conference of representatives of governments at the level of heads of state or government, meeting on 29 october 1993. moreover, as regards the action taken by the greek government in case t-120/99, the council takes note of the judgment of the court of first instance in that case, which dismissed the appeal and the pleas raised by the applicant against the language arrangements of that office.
- madam president of the council you are, as we are accustomed to hearing from you, extremely polite to the european parliament. you are very courteous in referring to article 308. we all know that article 308 is a unanimous decision of the council after parliament has given its opinion. so you're waiting to make a clear statement. i understand that, but nevertheless we must all be aware that it is apparently the case that only a limited number of member states, and i mention portugal, i mention spain, i mention greece, i mention finland, i mention italy, really oppose the exclusive use of french, english and german. all the other member states, and unfortunately my country too, seem to agree that the language arrangements are applied very narrowly. i would ask you, madam president-in-office, if it would not be better to ensure that the key elements of such a patent, the conclusions which are barely two or three pages long, are at least made available in all the languages. this would considerably increase legal certainty for citizens and businesses in the european union. then you don't have to translate 20 or 30 pages, just the conclusions. i think we would bring europe a little closer to the citizen.
- president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the creation of a genuine internal market for services will give a further boost to innovation for small and mediumsized enterprises - as has already been stressed in the debate - will improve the international competitiveness of european industry and provide greater benefits to consumers who will be offered a whole range of competitive high-quality services. i therefore believe it is essential that the internal market strategy builds on the strength of the e-commerce directive and the principle of mutual recognition. mutual recognition is the basis of the european union and, more specifically, of the european single market. mutual recognition can only be achieved if consumers enjoy equivalent protection throughout the union. this means that harmonisation is required only if this equivalent protection does not exist. moreover, a company established in the union is guaranteed complete freedom to trade in services across borders. i therefore believe that the report as it has now been adopted in the committee on legal affairs, after adding, among other things, a number of amendments which i myself have tabled, reflects and supports the balanced approach of the commission. finally, i am very pleased to hear that yesterday the commission adopted a regulation on promotion. this shows that the commission is genuinely interested in further developing the internal market. we cannot afford in the present economic circumstances to let this momentum to a genuine internal market slip by.
- mrs pack has rightly pointed out that we still have a lot of problems ahead of us. but all in all, the peace process in macedonia can be considered a half-miracle. and i would like to join mr haarder in congratulating the macedonian government and in pointing out the courage of that government. it is true, ladies and gentlemen, that a gordian knot has been cut. the minority rights of the albanians have been guaranteed, operation essential harvest has been a success, and the sovereignty and territorial integrity of macedonia remain guaranteed. the multiethnic nature of society is indeed given a new lease of life and dialogue between the parties is central. and we must all pay constant attention to this region, to the whole of the balkans. the crisis in afghanistan, the fight against terrorism must not distract us from this part of the world. we must invest in the stability of the balkans. we must invest in the stability of macedonia. we must ensure that adequate assistance is provided for the implementation of the stabilisation and association agreement. we must ensure that the refugees who return are properly accompanied. we have to rebuild the country. and i would also like to emphasize the words of mrs pack. the political history of my country has shown that only forgiveness, only reconciliation can bring true peace to a country. and so amnesty for the rebels is indeed important. it is not about the rebels who may be brought before the court in the hague. it's about the others, and those rebels need to be dealt with. i hope that our colleagues who are leaving for skopje tomorrow will be able to draw sufficient attention to this problem among their colleagues there. i also fully support the extension of the mandate of the european agency for reconstruction in the region. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
i would like to say that i fully agree with what mr swoboda has said. i am the chairman of the european parliament's delegation for relations with uzbekistan. let me give a personal testimony today. last june we spent a week in uzbekistan with a delegation from the european parliament. we have visited the region of karakalpakstan, the region where the great problems of the aral sea are being faced. colleagues, the situation there is dramatic. commissioner, may i have your attention, please? we met the people there. we met with the rescue workers. they thanked our delegation very much, they addressed us, they gave us messages, but they also said to us: we have seen so many delegations come here. when will the water problem in our region finally be addressed? when will the european community, when will the entire international community help us to do something together with the other countries in this region? the situation there is dramatic, including in the area of public health. i visited the local hospital in nukus with my colleague wiebenga, where tb patients are treated. the conditions there are inhuman. the sewer line in the basement is broken. it's all filled with human excrement. the mosquitoes are flying around. the stench is unbearable. the patients have to keep the flies and the mosquitoes out of their bodies, and that means that patients who normally have to stay there for three months are gone. they're going home. they infected their relatives. they infected other people in their vicinity. i believe that we as an international community must take responsibility. i hope therefore that the commissioner will be able to promise us some concrete measures in the near future. i fully support the text of the resolution before us.
mr president, on behalf of my group i would like to thank mr goebbels for all the energy and intellectual investment he has put into his report. we regret that the motion for a resolution before us is in the end different from the explanatory statement he had drawn up, and that is why we thought it appropriate to introduce a few amendments to reinforce the political message that could be given to the report. the first category of amendments concerns the 'organisational' aspect of the international monetary fund, whose situation seems to us to be unacceptable at the moment. that is why we are taking up an idea put forward by jacques delors, namely that of an economic and social security council within the united nations. we would like the commission and the council to make a statement on this at the council meeting in barcelona next spring. we also believe that the decision-making process and the composition of the international monetary fund's board of directors should be substantially revised. the second major chapter on which we wish to speak concerns the need to combat terrorism, which includes an extremely important financial dimension. that is why we feel that there is an additional incentive to make the fatf recommendations binding and to extend its mandate, as the european council in ghent has called for. i think that would complete the report. finally, as regards capital flows - and more specifically foreign exchange transactions - i am no longer speaking personally of a tobin tax, but i am still calling for a thorough debate on taxes which could dissuade speculative attacks on the currencies of a number of countries. i think that from this point of view there are some interesting proposals which should be supported.

- mr president, i should like to draw the attention of the commission and of parliament to two points in connection with the present evaluation of the employment process. first, the method of coordination itself. it has become a sought-after method in the social sector, and this for a range of issues which fall within national competence but which create common problems in all european countries. so common objectives can be set. but the success of the method, commissioner, given that it is not a coercive mechanism, depends on the moral and political pressure that can be exerted. media coverage plays a vital role in this. the open method of coordination has been in operation for five years now and there has been a very strong erosion of attention. the media attention is relaxing and, as a result, the pressure on national governments to take seriously the objectives and the annual criticism of their national action plan by the council and the commission is diminishing. the open method of coordination is only successful as long as the stress of convergence persists and efforts will have to be made to maintain it. so the question is how to keep the pressure on the boiler and i think this should be one of the crucial points in the evaluation. and then a second point, best practices. the various reports have listed best practices for the national member states. the question is: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. how many member states have visited projects of other member states? how many best practices have been discussed in the different member states? it is one of the methods of making progress in employment policy even if the pressure on the boiler is reduced. i therefore ask that the evaluation should examine these two points.

- madam president, as a native of ghent, i should like first of all to express my satisfaction at the peaceful conduct of the demonstrations following the european council in ghent. what went wrong in gothenburg and genoa did not go wrong in ghent. this is undoubtedly due to the change in the timetable since 9/11, but also to the good preparation, the judicious approach of the police and certainly also to the open dialogue that has led to a different atmosphere. the original purpose of this informal summit - mr verhofstadt has already stressed this - was to prepare the laeken declaration. the fifteen have ratified the lines of force and the timetable for the convention. as a european parliament we must dare to admit that this is a real show of strength. six months ago, this was absolutely unthinkable. it is also positive that the union has shown unity and determination in the fight against terrorism, has unequivocally affirmed its solidarity with the usa, but at the same time has emphasised its own political role. so, strictly speaking, ghent has lived up to expectations. but the way in which european unity has been achieved has once again exposed the weaknesses of the european construction. gent has also painfully demonstrated that the double tension of nice, between the big and the small countries, between the intergovernmental approach and the community approach, still seriously jeopardizes the future of the european union. that applies a priori to the cfsp. the french president is formally correct when he says that the european union has no competence in military matters. but the question is whether people are prepared to change that. the three largest member states must realise that they cannot keep one foot in and the other out of the union. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. let us hope, madam president, that 11 september and the need to reform the institutions before enlargement will lead the governments of the member states to look beyond their own national policies and to return to the roots of the european union, in particular through intensive cooperation between the fifteen, and not between three member states, to promote peace and security in europe and in the world.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i am going to present to you the resolution adopted by the committee on development under a special procedure - the hughes procedure - which is closely linked to fisheries policy, and i would like to say that the resolution fully incorporates the opinion of the committee on fisheries. in this report and in this resolution we have started from a twofold observation: firstly, in the developing countries, the respective contribution of coastal communities to food security and employment is a driving force for the sustainable development of these regions and the reduction of poverty. secondly, and this is unfortunately less positive, there is a permanent decline in fish stocks in the fishing areas of these developing countries. the committee on development and cooperation considers that the principles of the fao code of conduct for responsible fisheries should be applied in fisheries relations between the european union and developing countries. i would like to recall the main elements of this code: application of the precautionary principle to resource management, priority to the needs of the coastal populations of developing countries, and in particular to small-scale and artisanal fishing, participation of the actors directly concerned, that is to say local populations, and finally cooperation at subregional, regional and global level to promote the conservation and management of resources. the committee on development and cooperation has proposed a number of important strategic elements to ensure coherence between the sectoral policies of the european union and development policy. i am thinking of course in particular of fisheries policy, but also of regional policy. the first element proposed in the resolution is that development policy should pay greater attention to the fisheries sector by drawing up documents for a country-specific cooperation strategy based on the political dialogue between the european union and the country concerned. secondly, improving the living conditions of coastal communities must be taken into account in the various priority areas of intervention defined in the cooperation strategy. thirdly, specific mechanisms must be put in place to strengthen the organizations of the fisheries sector, and in particular of the fisheries processing sector. i am thinking of women's organizations, which are extremely important in developing countries and have relatively few resources to operate. fourthly, the application of the precautionary principle means, in the context of fisheries agreements, a serious prior scientific assessment of available resources. in the event of doubt, the precautionary principle should be applied, i.e. the lowest possible assumption should be used. fifthly, it is important that the european union should support monitoring and control programmes in the exclusive economic zones of developing countries. we all know that a number of these countries do not have the technical means to carry out these checks and to exercise this supervision. it is therefore important that the european union should contribute to this, to prevent overfishing and the piracy of certain vessels. in this spirit, we must also help these countries to combat flags of convenience and other vessels that illegally fish in their exclusive economic zones. finally, in the resolution we ask the council to amend the ifop so that subsidies to community shipowners who transfer their vessels to flags of convenience are stopped. i have not touched on all the points, but my colleague mr varela, from the committee on fisheries, will be pleased to highlight those which have been particularly proposed by his committee.

the rapporteur. - mr president, when i became a member of this parliament two and a half years ago, i immediately asked commissioner reding what she intended to do about the precarious situation of european cinema. as a filmmaker, i have always wondered how long europe would tolerate the gradual decline of what is, after all, a substantial part of our cultural heritage. indeed, in the 1960s european films still accounted for 60 per cent of the european market. now, sadly, that percentage has dropped to 22 percent. that is an absolute historical low. european films only make 26% of their revenues outside their own country. that's certainly far too little. the trade deficit with the united states has therefore reached - and i quote this figure very gladly because it is so immense - eur 6 829 million. that is a huge sum and we must be able to reduce europe's trade deficit with america. europe, however, makes more films than america, perhaps even better than america. that's a very subjective thing to say. but it is a pity that it is particularly difficult to see a film from another european country in one's own country. it's very difficult to watch a spanish film in greece. it is very difficult to see an italian film in the netherlands, with a few exceptions. i think it would be shortsighted to advocate large-scale subsidies for european cinema. i think that there are enough forces in europe, creative forces, forces of actors, of authors but also of financiers, which will make it possible to revive european cinema. that is why european cinema does not need any help. we just need the resources and the environment to be creative again. i therefore also call for a very ambitious european project. i think we must first of all examine the fundamental weakness of european cinema, and that is not at all in the creative but rather in the financial aspect. european entrepreneurs wishing to invest in european films have particular difficulties in obtaining, for example, bank guarantees to ensure that their investments are not too risky. in america, the financial strength of the studios makes this a much less problematic issue. hence, there is also a kind of capital flight from european capital to america. we must try to reverse this trend. i think that the european investment bank can be expected to help by guaranteeing itself to banks which in turn want to help those who want to invest in europe. that money is needed to give european producers the means to strengthen european cinema and to overcome its weaknesses - and i am talking about the lack of development. european films are not sufficiently developed. americans think longer, work longer on films before they release them. we don't have the money for that. we can also learn a great deal from the americans in the field of promotion. even if we have learned it, we lack the means to carry out that promotion and marketing. i am convinced that if we can support producers in this area, this can change. i also think we should think, for example, of the structural funds. the structural funds can be used to strengthen smaller cinemas in less developed areas. that's a road that hasn't been trodden yet. i would like to see the commission consider this. i would also like to talk about how we are going to protect our heritage. unfortunately, many films are lost. the memory of european cinema is being lost. europe should also be ambitious in this area, because it is very important for the education of our children that young people can still see these films. i shall stick to this, because i make some thirty recommendations in this report. i would also like to thank the members of the committee on culture for the very constructive way in which we have worked together and i hope that this will lead to approval tomorrow.

- mr president, commissioner, in the light of the decisions taken in recent years by the council and the european parliament on the opening up to competition of sectors which were previously protected, i.e. monopolies, it has become clear that we must be able to have a solid legal basis to guarantee and develop services of general economic interest which are effectively accessible to all and which thus embody the quality of being a citizen of the european union. the current economic situation and the many job losses recorded in recent weeks, as well as the fact that, even in a period of high growth, in recent years average unemployment rates of the order of 8% have been observed, underline the absolute need, in our opinion, to develop services of general interest. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. a first step at european level was taken with article 16 of the treaty of amsterdam. it is now considered insufficient, and that is why we support the draft framework directive and the amendments tabled by the european socialist party, the gue and the greens. we expect the laeken council, which will include a section on the objectives of the union, to state clearly this objective on services of general interest and the framework directive, together with a timetable. the stakes are high. the aim, as in the case of public procurement procedures - the zappala report - and as in the case of a particular sector - public passenger transport, the meijer report - is to establish a framework for european competition policy, to develop a regulated competition policy which effectively enables national, regional or local public authorities, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, to decide on the tasks of services of general interest, the management contract to be fulfilled by operators and the financing of these tasks. that is the meaning of amendments nos 52 and 55 which we tabled. we also think that it is necessary to insist that much more thorough evaluation work be carried out on the liberalization of the sectors, taking into account the interests of consumers and workers as well as the compatibility of the market trends with the objectives of sustainable development - i am thinking, for example, of the water sector - and this not only from a technical point of view, but also through a dialogue with all the actors concerned. these are amendments nos 44 and 46. the fact that these various amendments have had to be tabled in plenary shows clearly that there is no unanimity on what i have just mentioned in terms of objectives and, above all, the means to be used. once again, in this field as in others, what we need is more european integration, respecting the community method and guaranteeing the certainty of action of public actors at all levels to ensure the provision of services of general interest.

- madam president, the bankruptcy of the belgian airline sabena a few days ago is not only the biggest bankruptcy in belgian history, but first and foremost a human and social drama for 7500 to perhaps 12000 employees. it has already been said here and it is obvious that sabena is not an isolated case. there are many airlines that are going through very difficult times, and the events of september 11 have not improved the situation. i know that, but as far as the sabena case is concerned, the president-in-office of the council, mrs durant, has nevertheless withheld two very fundamental points from this parliament. first and foremost: sabena has been losing money every year for 40 years, except for one year, and that was probably an accounting trick. the taxpayers have been allowed to pump 62 billion francs into that company over the past 40 years. secondly: the cause. sabena was a belgian state-owned company with all the characteristics of the belgian state and the socialist party. that meant political profiteering, political appointments of manifestly incompetent people and political interference by the belgian royal family to keep the company in french-speaking hands. for this reason, the alliance with klm was sabotaged. everybody in my country knows that. the house of representatives is currently setting up a parliamentary committee of inquiry to examine this years of mismanagement, and in my country such things do not happen lightly. the airline industry is going through difficult times, i agree, and we must find solutions in europe on the basis of a policy of 'europe first' as long as the americans say 'america first'. in the sabena case, however, the first thing to do is to look for those responsible where they are to be found, namely at the belgian royal house and at the successive belgian governments.

in writing. - the vote in the committee on the environment on this report is a clear message to industry and national authorities for a flawless control of chemical substances, which must be accompanied by the elimination of toxic and harmful substances for health and the environment. in this sense, we generally support the philosophy of this report, which improves on a number of points the commission's proposal, which is itself ambitious. however, we feel that to be heard, our message must also be balanced. that is why we support a pragmatic approach and reject a series of unrealistic requirements which are a serious handicap for the european chemical industry. we must not forget that this represents no less than 5 million direct and indirect jobs. firstly, paragraph 16, which requires registration of all substances weighing less than one tonne: 100 000 products instead of the 30 000 proposed by the commission, tens of thousands of dossiers, an unbelievable amount of bureaucracy, hell for the many smes in the sector, and for products which, for the most part, never reach the market! the extension of the scope of the authorization procedure (paragraph 38) is equally impracticable: in order not to end up in an unmanageable situation, we ask that substances for which there is no evidence of toxicity be excluded from this scope (our amendment no 68). in short, if the message is to be strong, it must also be credible. we do not think it is appropriate or productive to declare war on industry, workers and national authorities.
- i just want to say that three years after hurricane mitch, central america is, with hurricane michelle, hit by another tragedy. since then, it has been plagued by earthquakes, droughts and famines. nevertheless, despite these disasters, democracy is slowly being restored in these countries, and the european delegation, of which i was a member, which has just visited guatemala, honduras and nicaragua, is unanimous on this point. the recent regularity of elections in nicaragua is also a testimony to this. i therefore believe that the european union has played a decisive role in this democratic development, and we can be proud of that. that is why i am asking for two things: first, to increase the amount of appropriations allocated to the reconstruction plan for central america, which currently amounts to eur 250 million for the period 1999-2002, since no new disasters have been taken into account. the second is to review the anachronistic position of the european union vis-à-vis cuba. cuba, which has also been completely devastated by this new hurricane, the worst it has experienced in 50 years, is currently the only country in central america which has not concluded a cooperation agreement with the european union and can only rely on humanitarian and emergency aid in these situations of distress. so, as far as cuba is concerned, i could tell you, with a smile, not to be more catholic than the pope, knowing that he has not hesitated on the choice to make, but the tragic situation of this country does not allow me to smile. and it's more seriously that i say "don't mistake the enemy". the enemy is no longer communism, but terrorism, and terrorism finds its breeding ground in misery; - the president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, mr rapporteur, the regulation before us is, in my view, revolutionary. i do believe that crises can be a purifying force. in the food sector too, we often see that crises lead to legislative measures being put forward. the regulation before us is a step forward. i would like to stress a number of points which were particularly important to my group and which we have also taken up. firstly, the scheme will ensure that contaminated food can no longer be exported to third countries, often third world countries. the second part emphasizes the diversity of food, points out that there are traditional products, that we must recognize this and that food has a certain cultural role to play. the third point is that we as parliament have made sure that we have turned the planned food authority into a food safety authority, and that is a major victory for our parliament. parliament, together with your fellow rapporteur, has ensured that we have been able to play a part and to weigh in on the transparency of this whole thing. it is true that we need transparency to restore consumer confidence. we have also, as a parliament, made sure that we have a better understanding of the composition of the management board and the appointment of the director. finally, we also called for more objective criteria for the seat. when we came out of the committee on the environment at second reading, we saw that we had 28 amendments, 28 amendments which were sometimes difficult to get through to the council. we have had a sort of pre-conciliation exercise, an exercise which i am very pleased about, because the rapporteur, together with the co-rapporteurs and the shadow rapporteurs, has ensured that we as parliament have been able to speak with one voice in front of the council, a council where we have heard a voice, where mrs aelvoet has listened to us and i am very pleased about that.

- mr president of the commission, madam president-in-office of the council, i should like to make a few comments on the subject which is at the heart of today's debate: the commission's legislative and work programme. mr president of the commission, you have told us that you want full and transparent cooperation between the commission and parliament. that's very good. you said to us: 'to be strong, the european parliament needs a strong commission'. we agree. but you will understand that we cannot accept being treated as more or less docile pariahs of the commission. we fully agree, of course, that it is important that the work programme should be politically readable, as is the case here; this does not, however, exempt you from presenting a legislative programme. but we are faced today with a work programme that was given to us at the last minute and a legislative programme that we have only known about since last night. it's difficult for us to work under such conditions. the european parliament already suffers from having no right of legislative initiative, even in the case of a commission defect - which, in my view, is an institutional anomaly that should be corrected to fill the democratic deficit. however, it enjoys active cooperation powers recognised by the commission. i would remind you that since the beginning of this parliament we have had a framework agreement with the commission which clearly defines the role of parliament in the legislative process. in accordance with this agreement, the 2002 legislative programme should have been presented to us last october, together with an evaluation of the previous programme; we should have been able to make criticisms, comments and proposals at a more politically appropriate time and within a reasonable time. it is important to stress the political relevance of the mutually reinforcing and complementary political debates on the budget and the legislative programme. october or november is a good time for this. today we are two months behind schedule and we have no legislative programme, or rather we have only just discovered it. in these circumstances, i consider that parliament is de facto deprived of its power of control and influence, which is not a good thing. i would also like to mention a few concrete facts: on 30 november, just a few days ago, less than half of the 2001 programme had been implemented. in addition, half of the initiatives taken were not included in the 2001 legislative programme. this is surprising to say the least: it is easy to assume that the unforeseen events that have upset the political agenda justified the submission of proposals to take account of these upheavals, but a careful reading of the texts shows that, for the most part, these new proposals have nothing to do with current events or, in any case, could have been tabled at another time than in the context of the 2001 programme. this, in my view, reveals - and i think most of my group will agree - a rather great disorder within the commission and a lack of a common vision of the guidelines which should guide the joint work of our institutions. the explanations you give us to justify this, which are linked to the internal reforms of the commission, cannot satisfy us forever. i would point out that these explanations have been useful since the beginning of the legislature. we cannot accept, mr president, that the european parliament is deprived of its clearly recognized power of control; the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. when we talk about good governance, about reconciliation with the citizen, the least we can do is to ensure that the commitments made are respected and that parliament's prerogatives are also respected.
mr president, i would just like to say that i was very pleased to vote for the amendments to the common position on food safety, for which mr whitehead was rapporteur. i would like to thank the honourable member for his great efforts, because it was thanks to his work that we were able to reach clear, clear and good agreements across the groups and that is why we are where we are today. we have adopted only those amendments today which have the full support of the commission. agreements have already been made with the council. this means that the food authority can be operational in 2002, which means that we are fully on schedule. i think we can safely say that this is due to good cooperation, across the groups, in this house. we've created a framework that has to work. if we can put the right men and women in the right places and give the authority a seat that allows it to make very flexible external contacts, then i think that all the conditions are in place for this food authority to be a success. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1408/71 on the application of social security schemes to employed persons and their families moving within the community.

the commission, mr heaton-harris, two weeks ago we had a debate on the directive on the protection of the community's financial interests, and that directive defines the criminal offences of fraud, corruption and money laundering in the way that all the member states, including the united kingdom, had already agreed in 1995. but it has not yet entered into force, and for that reason the commission has proposed to incorporate it into a directive. the european parliament, like the commission, felt that this directive was not the right place to define a prosecutor, because, in the opinion of the commission and also of the parliament, the creation of a european prosecutor cannot be regulated in a directive, but requires a treaty amendment. that is why parliament has deleted a member's proposal to regulate this in the directive. but it was not a vote against the european public prosecutor. i think we need to record that here again. the european union has a duty to protect the rights of the defence. the first is that he is responsible for the direction and coordination of law enforcement activities and also for the conduct of investigations. the investigation itself would then be governed by national law. the european prosecutor would then, having collected evidence of the charge and the discharge, have the right to bring an action on the basis of that evidence, and the action would also be brought again under national law before a national court, so that it is not a question of creating a large new apparatus or creating a new european criminal code, because we know how much scepticism there is still about this. we have listed many of these cases in the green paper, and we have, for example, until now, had the rule that if evidence was collected in one member state and then, because the crime is transnational, it is to be presented in a transnational procedure before another court, then it is said, for example: no, that was collected by administrative law. we do not accept that. it is often the case that there is a great deal of evidence of fraud in the european budget, but the evidence is virtually - i do not want to say bureaucratically, but legally - suppressed because there are such differences. i think that is not in the interests of the citizens and it is not in the interests of the european parliament. i would ask you, mr heaton-harris, to examine this proposal with an open mind, because we have said that we are committed to applying the principle of subsidiarity, but we also want to have effective criminal law protection of the community's interests.

- mr president, commission, and ladies and gentlemen, there is a future for the world trade organization. the 144 member states will indeed be renegotiating the further liberalization of world trade in the coming years, but more than ever the rich man's club has found that the strongest no longer automatically wins the longest. the developing countries have indeed made a considerable profit and have left their mark on the negotiations. but the european union should also be pleased. thanks to the commitment and perseverance of the european negotiators, our two commissioners who are here, agreement has finally been reached on a broad round of negotiations. the new round will include negotiations on public procurement, competition, investment, the relationship between international environmental agreements and wto rules. these trade talks come at an important time, because they signal that member states are prepared to cooperate internationally after the terrorist attacks in the united states and that terrorists cannot undermine international trade. the continuation of the liberal trade policy is also important because it can give a major boost to our faltering world economy. finally, i would like to say that the challenge for the world trade organisation is to continue to develop the multilateral trading system in such a way that globalisation brings the greatest possible benefits to the greatest possible number of people and countries. we must continue the work begun more than 50 years ago.

and one only has to read the commission's texts again to see for oneself - that we, the rich, industrialized countries, have not succeeded in changing our production and consumption patterns to make them sustainable for the planet. we didn't make it. this means that strong initiatives must be taken in this respect. there was the gothenburg summit, which was promising, and there were some positive points, notably kyoto, which was saved, thanks to the positive action of the belgian presidency and the european commission, despite the negative attitude, once again, of the united states, but this kyoto protocol is never more than one tool among others, and even a rather disappointing one compared to the initial ambitions. i think that in any case we cannot rejoice in johannesburg over the concrete results we have achieved in the fight against the greenhouse effect, nor in the protection of biodiversity. there's a whole series of cases where we've actually kind of regressed. as for poverty in the world, mr president, you yourself said that the gap between the north and the south has not been reduced. it has actually tended to widen. so i think there is a considerable effort to be made at this level.
